ID: a1225373-e1ca-45c8-91bf-5df3f545318f
Q: When providing group therapy, the nurse must focus on:
A: Jointly experienced stress
B: Behaviour of individual members
C: Confrontation between members
D: Personal feelings affecting behaviour
Correct: A
Rationale: GROUP THERAPY (Yalom's curative factors) — the therapist focuses on the GROUP PROCESS and JOINTLY EXPERIENCED issues: universality ("we are not alone"), interpersonal learning, cohesion, altruism, catharsis, instillation of hope. The COMMON STRESS/EXPERIENCE shared by group members provides the basis for therapy — option A. Individual behaviour, confrontations and personal feelings are addressed but in the context of the GROUP dynamic.
----------------------------------------
ID: fe28c618-6009-471f-b8b6-20fe8b70fe85
Q: To prevent side effects associated with prolonged phenytoin use, a client should be educated to:
A: Avoid crowds and obtain influenza vaccination annually
B: Drink 2 litres of fluid daily including 6–8 glasses of water
C: Eat a potassium rich, low sodium diet
D: Observe good dental hygiene and report any gum bleeding
Correct: D
Rationale: A characteristic, distinctive long-term side effect of phenytoin is GINGIVAL HYPERPLASIA (overgrowth of gum tissue) — the patient needs meticulous oral hygiene, regular dental checks, and should report bleeding/swelling of gums. Other phenytoin side effects: hirsutism, coarsening of features, ataxia, nystagmus, megaloblastic anaemia (folate antagonism), osteomalacia, drug interactions (enzyme induction), Stevens-Johnson syndrome.
----------------------------------------

ID: 170de96a-d12c-44ec-98d9-9b46fdd91b9f
Q: The main goal of treatment for acute glomerulonephritis is:
A: Encourage activity
B: Encourage high protein diet
C: Maintain fluid balance
D: Teach intermittent urinary catheterisation
Correct: C
Rationale: Acute glomerulonephritis (post-streptococcal in children) presents with the nephritic syndrome: haematuria, hypertension, oedema, mild proteinuria and oliguria/azotaemia. Maintenance of FLUID BALANCE is central — fluid restriction, daily weights, monitoring intake/output and BP. Diet is LOW protein and LOW sodium during oliguric phase. Activity is restricted; catheterisation is not routinely needed.
----------------------------------------

ID: 22bde09e-26c7-476a-a818-58359775aa65
Q: The most common, preventable complication of abdominal surgery is:
A: Atelectasis
B: Fluid and electrolyte imbalance
C: Thrombophlebitis
D: Urinary retention
Correct: A
Rationale: Atelectasis (collapse of lung segments from poor ventilation and retained secretions) is the most common complication of any abdominal/thoracic surgery — pain inhibits coughing and deep breathing. It is highly PREVENTABLE through pre-op patient education, deep breathing and coughing exercises, incentive spirometry, early ambulation, adequate analgesia and chest physiotherapy.
----------------------------------------

Q: Early miscarriage refers to:

B: Spontaneous loss of pregnancy at 12 weeks gestation
C: Spontaneous loss of pregnancy from between 15 to 24 weeks gestation
D: Loss of pregnancy within the first trimester
Correct: D

ID: 25f555a8-1600-4742-9526-3361897c4a3a
Q: In myocardial infarction, morphine is administered to:
A: Dilate coronary blood vessels
B: Prevent fibrillation of the ventricles
C: Decrease anxiety and restlessness
D: Prevent shock and relieve pain
Correct: D
Rationale: In acute MI, morphine is given to: (i) relieve severe ischaemic chest PAIN (analgesic); (ii) reduce sympathetic catecholamine surge and ANXIETY which would otherwise increase myocardial O2 demand; (iii) venodilate to reduce preload and cardiac workload (helpful when MI is complicated by pulmonary oedema). All these effects together help PREVENT cardiogenic SHOCK and relieve pain — option D is the most complete summary.

B: Diazepam 0.3 mg/kg IV over 1 minute
C: Phenobarbitone 5 mg/kg IM stat
D: Diazepam 1 mg/kg per rectal stat

B: Decision making and judgement
C: Priority setting and expected outcomes formulation
D: Reassessment and audit

Q: The immediate management for severe dehydration in a child with severe malnutrition according to IMCI strategy includes:
A: Infusion of half-strength Darrow's in 5% dextrose 15 mL/kg IV in 1 hour
B: Infusion of 0.9% normal saline 20 mL/kg in 1 hour

Q: The ratio of infant chest compressions to ventilation where 2 rescuers are involved is:
A: 30:2
B: 30:1
C: 15:2
D: 15:1
Correct: C

Q: Which of the following is correct? (a) The iliac crest is the site of choice for bone marrow aspiration in a 5-year-old child. (b) Acute renal failure is the leading complication associated with acute glomerulonephritis.
A: Both (a) TRUE and (b) TRUE
B: Both (a) FALSE and (b) FALSE

D: (a) FALSE and (b) TRUE
Correct: C

Q: Haemophilus influenzae type B (Hib) vaccine is given to protect against:
A: Diphtheria and whooping cough
B: Hepatitis B and whooping cough
C: Hepatitis B and meningitis

Q: One of the following is NOT true of a malignant tumour. It is:
A: Invasive
B: Encapsulated
C: Frequently undifferentiated
D: Associated with paraneoplastic syndrome

Q: A child with acute lymphoblastic leukaemia has a high risk of infection because:
A: White blood cells are produced in inadequate numbers
B: The skin integrity is altered
C: There is a down-regulation of immune system by catecholamine
D: The white blood cells are immature
Correct: D
Rationale: ACUTE LYMPHOBLASTIC LEUKAEMIA (ALL) — uncontrolled proliferation of IMMATURE LYMPHOID CELLS (LYMPHOBLASTS) in bone marrow. Q: The following feature indicates excessive IV fluid infusion in a 3-year-old child:
A: Worsening dyspnoea
B: Gastric distension
C: Nausea and vomiting
D: Temperature of 102°F (38.9°C)
Correct: A
Rationale: FLUID OVERLOAD in children — children have less cardiovascular reserve and are more vulnerable to volume overload than adults. MANAGEMENT: stop/reduce fluids, diuretics (furosemide), oxygen, treat cause. Option B gastric distension — could be from many causes. Option D fever — not a fluid-overload sign.

Q: The primary purpose of administering corticosteroids to a child with nephrotic syndrome is to:
A: Increase blood pressure
B: Reduce inflammation
C: Decrease proteinuria
D: Prevent infection
Correct: C
Rationale: NEPHROTIC SYNDROME in children — characterised by: heavy PROTEINURIA (>40 mg/m²/hr), HYPOALBUMINAEMIA (<25 g/L), OEDEMA, HYPERLIPIDAEMIA. Commonest type in children = MINIMAL CHANGE DISEASE (80% steroid-responsive). FIRST-LINE TREATMENT: CORTICOSTEROIDS (prednisolone 60 mg/m²/day for 4-6 weeks then taper) — induce REMISSION by REDUCING PROTEINURIA — option C — primary therapeutic goal. Steroids work by reducing podocyte injury and stabilising glomerular filtration barrier. Other measures: salt and fluid restriction, diuretics if needed, ACE inhibitors, treat infections, thromboprophylaxis.

ID: 30f4c8d6-241b-4825-a16f-b707bceeb859
Q: Uncontrolled vomiting in an infant can result in:
A: Tetany
B: Acidosis
C: Alkalosis
D: Hypersensitivity
Correct: C
Rationale: PROLONGED VOMITING — loss of GASTRIC CONTENTS including HYDROCHLORIC ACID (H+ and Cl-) and POTASSIUM — leads to HYPOKALAEMIC, HYPOCHLORAEMIC METABOLIC ALKALOSIS (option C). The kidneys try to compensate but are limited by potassium depletion. Classic example: PYLORIC STENOSIS in 2-6 week old infants — projectile non-bilious vomiting → metabolic alkalosis. Diarrhoea would cause METABOLIC ACIDOSIS (loss of bicarbonate, option B). TETANY (option A) can occur in severe alkalosis (alkalosis reduces ionised calcium) but is downstream consequence, not the primary acid-base disturbance. Treatment: rehydration with normal saline + potassium replacement.

Q: In paediatric emergencies, priority signs include:
A: Poisoning, restlessness, coma
B: Pallor, pain, oedema of both feet
C: Convulsion, central cyanosis, poisoning
D: Pyrexia, severe respiratory distress, coma
Correct: C
Rationale: Per Kenya MoH/WHO ETAT (Emergency Triage Assessment and Treatment) for paediatrics — EMERGENCY SIGNS require IMMEDIATE intervention: obstructed/absent BREATHING, severe respiratory DISTRESS, central CYANOSIS, shock, COMA/convulsing now (CONVULSIONS), severe DEHYDRATION. PRIORITY SIGNS — need urgent attention: Tiny baby (<2 months), Temperature very high, Trauma/urgent surgical, Pallor (severe), Poisoning, Pain (severe), Respiratory distress, Restless/irritable/lethargic, Referral, Malnutrition, Oedema (of both feet), Burns. Option C — convulsion + central cyanosis + POISONING — mixes EMERGENCY (convulsion, cyanosis) with PRIORITY (poisoning). Option B — pallor + pain + bilateral foot oedema — all PRIORITY signs. ID: 831d4d7b-469f-4ee0-bdfa-c57f28fa978b
Q: Hyperopia in children:
A: Results from unequal curvature of the cornea
B: Results from an eyeball that is too long
C: Occurs due to rays of light being bent in different directions
D: Occurs due to rays of light being focused behind the retina
Correct: D
Rationale: REFRACTIVE ERRORS: HYPEROPIA (far-sightedness) — light rays from near objects FOCUS BEHIND the retina (option D) — usually because EYEBALL is TOO SHORT or cornea/lens lacks curvature. Children can often accommodate to overcome mild hyperopia → eye strain, headaches. Most children have mild hyperopia at birth which decreases with age. MYOPIA (short-sightedness) — light focuses IN FRONT of retina; eyeball too LONG (option B describes myopia). ASTIGMATISM — UNEQUAL CORNEAL CURVATURE causing light to focus at different points in different planes (option A). Correction: hyperopia — CONVEX (plus) lens; myopia — CONCAVE (minus) lens.

ID: 1d53bc1c-83ef-4cf8-878a-8f65849f1f34
Q: Features of first-degree burns in children include:
A: Pain, blistering
B: Erythema, blanching with pressure
C: Leathery appearance, blistering
D: Minimal swelling, erythema
Correct: B
Rationale: BURN CLASSIFICATION by depth: FIRST DEGREE (superficial — epidermis only) — ERYTHEMA (redness), PAIN, BLANCHES with pressure (option B — TRUE), NO BLISTERS, dry, heals 3-7 days without scarring. e.g. sunburn. SECOND DEGREE — SUPERFICIAL PARTIAL THICKNESS — pink/red, BLISTERS (option A), painful, moist, blanches; heals 1-3 weeks, minimal scarring. DEEP PARTIAL THICKNESS — red/white mottled, painful or decreased sensation, may blister; heals 3-6 weeks with scarring. THIRD DEGREE (full thickness) — WHITE, BROWN, CHARRED, LEATHERY (option C), DRY, painless (nerves destroyed), needs grafting. Burns surface area assessed by Lund-Browder chart in children.

ID: f1201eeb-a5a0-404c-8575-9dc9d73d3551
Q: The main cause of oedema in nephrotic syndrome is:
A: Dilatation of blood capillaries
B: Inflammatory processes
C: Decreased albumin in blood
D: Renal failure
Correct: C
Rationale: NEPHROTIC SYNDROME OEDEMA pathophysiology — TRADITIONAL UNDERFILL theory: heavy PROTEINURIA → HYPOALBUMINAEMIA (<25 g/L) → DECREASED PLASMA ONCOTIC PRESSURE → fluid leaks from vessels into interstitium (option C — classic explanation); reduced effective circulating volume → activation of RAAS → sodium and water retention → worsening oedema. OVERFILL theory (more recent) — primary tubular sodium retention drives oedema independent of albumin. Both contribute. Clinically: periorbital and gravity-dependent oedema, ascites, scrotal/labial oedema, sometimes pleural effusion. Treatment: corticosteroids (reduce proteinuria), salt/fluid restriction, diuretics, ACE inhibitors.

ID: e713d8ee-716c-4816-b046-832ba287b3a0
Q: In nephrotic syndrome, hypovolaemia leads to decreased:
A: Secretion of antidiuretic hormone
B: Oncotic pressure
C: Renal blood flow
D: Secretion of aldosterone
Correct: C
Rationale: NEPHROTIC SYNDROME pathophysiology — heavy proteinuria → hypoalbuminaemia → decreased plasma oncotic pressure → fluid shifts to interstitium → DECREASED EFFECTIVE CIRCULATING VOLUME (relative HYPOVOLAEMIA) → DECREASED RENAL BLOOD FLOW (option C — TRUE) → activation of compensatory responses: INCREASED renin-angiotensin-aldosterone (option D wrong — aldosterone INCREASES) → sodium retention; INCREASED ADH/vasopressin (option A wrong — ADH INCREASES) → water retention; sympathetic activation. These compensations cause sodium/water retention worsening oedema. Reduced renal blood flow can precipitate ACUTE KIDNEY INJURY in severe cases or with aggressive diuresis.

Q: While monitoring a child on blood transfusion, the nurse notes that the child has developed chills, fever and vomiting. The nurse should:
A: Stop transfusion immediately
B: Transfuse the blood more slowly
C: Place child flat on the bed
D: Give antihistamines
Correct: A
Rationale: BLOOD TRANSFUSION REACTIONS — chills, fever, vomiting are red-flag signs of a potential TRANSFUSION REACTION (could be acute haemolytic, febrile non-haemolytic, allergic, septic, TRALI). IMMEDIATE NURSING ACTIONS: (1) STOP THE TRANSFUSION IMMEDIATELY (option A — first action — could be life-threatening haemolytic reaction); (2) MAINTAIN IV ACCESS with normal saline (different bag); (3) Notify physician; (4) Recheck patient/blood identification; (5) Send blood bag + post-transfusion sample to lab; (6) Monitor vital signs closely; (7) Treat symptoms (antipyretics, antihistamines, fluids, steroids, vasopressors as needed); (8) Document and report. NEVER slow the transfusion (option B — continued exposure dangerous).

Q: The priority nursing diagnosis for an infant with oesophageal atresia is:
A: Risk for aspiration
B: Impaired nutrition: less than body requirement
C: Pain
D: Altered perfusion
Correct: A
Rationale: OESOPHAGEAL ATRESIA (OA) — congenital anomaly where the oesophagus ends in a blind pouch; usually associated with TRACHEO-OESOPHAGEAL FISTULA (TOF, ~85% of cases). Newborn presents with EXCESSIVE FROTHY SECRETIONS, coughing/choking with feeds, cyanosis, respiratory distress. CRITICAL RISK = ASPIRATION — secretions cannot pass to stomach and accumulate in pouch → spill into airway; with TOF, gastric contents reflux into trachea via fistula. PRIORITY nursing diagnosis = RISK FOR ASPIRATION (option A) → airway/breathing is the immediate Maslow's basic need at risk. Interventions: NPO, continuous suction of pouch (Replogle tube), head elevated, IV fluids, antibiotics, prepare for urgent surgical repair.

Q: Middle ear inflammation is common in children. This mainly affects:
A: Short, horizontal Eustachian tube
B: Thin tympanic membrane
C: Permeable tympanic membrane
D: Short, vertical Eustachian tube
Correct: A
Rationale: OTITIS MEDIA (middle ear inflammation) is COMMONEST in children due to anatomical factors: EUSTACHIAN TUBE in CHILDREN is SHORTER, more HORIZONTAL, and WIDER (option A) than in adults (where it is longer and angled DOWNWARD at ~45°). This anatomy allows easier ascent of nasopharyngeal organisms into the middle ear. Plus IMMATURE IMMUNE SYSTEM, frequent URIs, adenoid hypertrophy obstructing tube, allergies, bottle-feeding while lying flat. Eustachian tube becomes more vertical and longer by ~7-8 years. Q: Nursing interventions for a 9-year-old child with immunosuppression include:
A: Informing the guardian about the condition, catheterisation to monitor urine output
B: Offering large amounts of fresh fruits and vegetables, nursing the child in isolation
C: Encouraging child to perform at optimal level, nursing the child in isolation
D: Nursing the child in isolation, catheterisation to monitor urine output
Correct: C
Rationale: Nursing IMMUNOSUPPRESSED child (chemotherapy, transplant, advanced HIV, congenital immunodeficiency, severe nephrotic): PROTECTIVE/REVERSE ISOLATION — single room, restricted visitors, careful hand hygiene, no fresh flowers/uncooked fruits and vegetables (raw foods carry pathogens — option B WRONG), no live vaccines, mask if leaving room; ENCOURAGE OPTIMAL FUNCTION — psychosocial support, normal age-appropriate activities WITHIN safety limits, school work, family involvement, play (option C TRUE — captures both isolation and optimal functioning); monitor for infection signs; good nutrition (well-cooked); skin/mouth care; AVOID INVASIVE PROCEDURES like CATHETERISATION unless absolutely indicated (introduce infection risk — options A, D wrong).

Q: Hypokalaemia is a condition characterised by low:
A: Blood calcium levels
B: Magnesium in the blood
C: Potassium in the blood
D: Sodium levels in the blood
Correct: C
Rationale: HYPO-KAL-EMIA = LOW POTASSIUM (kalium = potassium; K+) in blood (serum K <3.5 mmol/L). Causes: diarrhoea, vomiting, diuretics (loop, thiazides), aldosteronism, refeeding syndrome, insulin therapy, alkalosis. Features: muscle weakness, cramps, paralytic ileus, paraesthesia, ECG changes (flat T waves, U waves, ST depression, prolonged PR), arrhythmias. Q: The stage of syphilis characterised by the presence of gumma lesions is:
A: Primordial
B: Latent
C: Tertiary
D: Secondary
Correct: C
Rationale: Syphilis stages: PRIMARY (3 weeks post-infection — painless chancre at site of inoculation, regional adenopathy); SECONDARY (6 weeks-6 months — generalised rash including palms/soles, condylomata lata, mucous patches, systemic features); LATENT (asymptomatic, serologically positive — early <2 yrs, late >2 yrs); TERTIARY (years later — GUMMAS = chronic granulomatous lesions of skin, bone, viscera; CARDIOVASCULAR syphilis = aortitis, aneurysms; NEUROSYPHILIS = tabes dorsalis, general paresis). Q: The recommended second-line treatment of urethritis in an adult is:
A: Norfloxacin 800 mg stat plus doxycycline 100 mg BD for 7 days
B: Norfloxacin 800 mg twice a day for 7 days and spectinomycin 2 mg IM stat
C: Erythromycin 100 mg BD for 14 days and ceftriaxone 62.5 mg IM stat
D: Spectinomycin 2 g IM stat and doxycycline 100 mg BD for 7 days

Q: Clinical features of typhoid fever in the first week following the prodromal phase include:
A: Meningism, constipation, enlarged spleen
B: Pea-soup diarrhoea, meningism, stepwise rise in temperature
C: Constipation, abdominal distension, broncho-pneumonia
D: Mental confusion, enlarged spleen, pea-soup diarrhoea
Correct: B
Rationale: Typhoid fever (Salmonella typhi) clinical course: WEEK 1 — STEPWISE/staircase rise in temperature (rises each day, falls each morning), headache, malaise, dry cough; relative bradycardia (Faget's sign); pea-soup DIARRHOEA or constipation; sometimes meningism. WEEK 2 — sustained high fever, rose spots on trunk, HEPATOSPLENOMEGALY, mental confusion ("typhoid state"). WEEK 3 — complications: INTESTINAL PERFORATION, haemorrhage, myocarditis, encephalopathy. WEEK 4 — convalescence. Diagnosis: blood culture (best week 1), Widal (week 2). Treatment: ciprofloxacin/ceftriaxone.

Q: Progestin-only pills work primarily by:
A: Thickening cervical mucus, thus blocking sperm from meeting an egg
B: Causing a chemical change that damages sperms and eggs before they can meet
C: Suppressing the release of gonadotrophins
D: Plugging the fallopian tubes thus preventing egg from meeting sperm
Correct: A
Rationale: Mechanism of action of PROGESTIN-ONLY PILLS (POPs/mini-pills): PRIMARY action is THICKENING OF CERVICAL MUCUS (making it hostile to sperm penetration); also thins the endometrium (reduces implantation); slows tubal motility; inhibits ovulation in ~50% of cycles (variable — unlike combined COCs which reliably suppress ovulation). Because cervical-mucus effect is the primary mechanism and is time-sensitive, POPs must be taken at the SAME TIME EACH DAY (window only 3 hours). Option A is the correct primary mechanism.

Q: Growth and development increases in a rapid spurt at ages of:
A: 2 to 5 years
B: 9 to 12 years
C: 6 to 12 months
D: 5 to 8 years
Correct: B
Rationale: Periods of RAPID GROWTH (growth spurts) in human development: (1) INFANCY 0-12 months — first major spurt (triples birth weight by 12 months); (2) ADOLESCENCE — second major spurt: girls 9-13 years (peak velocity ~12 years), boys 11-15 years (peak ~14 years). Period 9-12 years aligns with pre-pubertal/early-pubertal spurt particularly in girls. Toddler/preschool/early-school years (1-9 years) is steady but slower growth. Option B fits the adolescent spurt.

A: Profuse watery stool preceding vomiting
B: High fever with severe abdominal cramps
C: Vomiting preceding profuse watery stool
D: Decreased urine production preceding profuse watery stool

Q: Amoebic dysentery:

B: Presents with very severe tenesmus
C: May present with bottle-shaped ulcers on the intestinal wall
D: Has an epidemic occurrence

Q: Drugs that are known to reduce the efficacy of hormonal contraceptives are:
A: Artemether lumefantrine, rifampicin
B: Pyrazinamide, phenytoin
C: Phenytoin, rifampicin
D: Ethambutol, co-trimoxazole
Correct: C
Rationale: CYP3A4 ENZYME INDUCERS reduce the plasma concentration and efficacy of hormonal contraceptives (especially combined OCs): RIFAMPICIN, RIFABUTIN (TB drugs — major inducers); ANTI-EPILEPTICS — PHENYTOIN, carbamazepine, phenobarbitone, primidone, topiramate; St John's wort; some antiretrovirals (efavirenz, nevirapine). D: 12 hours

ID: 15ed82e5-4384-451d-943a-5c0e340d5ce7
Q: A patient with asthma is having an acute attack. The nurse should administer:
A: Oral prednisolone as the first-line treatment
B: Salbutamol (albuterol) via nebuliser or inhaler as the first-line bronchodilator
C: Aminophylline IV as the first-line treatment
D: Ipratropium bromide alone as the first-line treatment
Correct: B
Rationale: In acute asthma, SHORT-ACTING BETA-2 AGONISTS (SABA) — SALBUTAMOL (albuterol) — are the FIRST-LINE bronchodilator. Administer via metered-dose inhaler (MDI) with spacer (2–4 puffs every 20 minutes for 3 doses) or nebuliser (2.5–5 mg). Add ipratropium bromide (anticholinergic) for moderate-severe attacks. Systemic corticosteroids (prednisolone oral or hydrocortisone IV) are given early to reduce airway inflammation but take 4–6 hours to work. Aminophylline is a third-line agent for severe/life-threatening asthma. Oxygen to maintain SpO2 94–98%.

ID: cce69aac-6524-478c-9414-eff12ab48db3
Q: A patient with heart failure is prescribed furosemide. The nurse should monitor for which electrolyte imbalance?
A: Hyperkalaemia
B: Hypernatraemia
C: Hypokalaemia
D: Hypercalcaemia
Correct: C
Rationale: FUROSEMIDE is a loop diuretic that inhibits the Na-K-2Cl cotransporter in the thick ascending limb of the loop of Henle, causing excretion of sodium, potassium, chloride, and water. The most important electrolyte to monitor is POTASSIUM — furosemide causes HYPOKALAEMIA (K+ <3.5 mmol/L). Hypokalaemia potentiates digoxin toxicity and causes cardiac arrhythmias, muscle weakness, and cramps. Management: potassium-rich diet (bananas, oranges, potatoes), potassium supplements, or potassium-sparing diuretics (spironolactone). Also monitor sodium, magnesium, and renal function.

ID: ad18a7c6-63e7-4e92-a8fe-9fe5dd08b13e
Q: A patient with peptic ulcer disease is prescribed omeprazole. The mechanism of action of this drug is:
A: It neutralises gastric acid in the stomach
B: It blocks H2 receptors on parietal cells to reduce acid secretion
C: It inhibits the proton pump (H+/K+ ATPase) on parietal cells
D: It coats the ulcer to protect it from acid
Correct: C
Rationale: OMEPRAZOLE is a PROTON PUMP INHIBITOR (PPI). It irreversibly inhibits the H+/K+ ATPase (proton pump) on the luminal surface of gastric parietal cells — the final common pathway of acid secretion. This produces the most potent and sustained acid suppression of any drug class. PPIs are first-line for peptic ulcer disease, GORD, H. pylori eradication (triple therapy), and Zollinger-Ellison syndrome. H2 blockers (ranitidine, famotidine) block histamine H2 receptors. Antacids (magnesium hydroxide) neutralise existing acid. Sucralfate coats the ulcer.

ID: 01b9f885-e7d3-4d66-b34b-e1611a65d5d9
Q: A patient with a urinary tract infection (UTI) is prescribed trimethoprim. The nurse should advise the patient to:
A: Take the medication with antacids to reduce stomach upset
B: Complete the full course of antibiotics even if symptoms resolve
C: Stop the medication as soon as symptoms improve
D: Take the medication only when symptoms are severe
Correct: B
Rationale: Antibiotic adherence is critical: patients must COMPLETE THE FULL COURSE of antibiotics even when symptoms resolve (usually within 1–2 days). Stopping early allows surviving bacteria (which may be more resistant) to multiply, causing relapse and contributing to antibiotic resistance. For uncomplicated UTI in women, trimethoprim is typically prescribed for 3–7 days. Advise: take at regular intervals, drink plenty of fluids, avoid sexual intercourse until treatment is complete, and return if symptoms worsen or do not resolve within 48 hours.

ID: bd334a94-5c7b-49ac-8a92-5bb7f5667991
Q: A doctor orders amoxicillin 500 mg. The available stock is amoxicillin 250 mg/5 mL suspension. How many mL should the nurse administer?
A: 5 mL
B: 10 mL
C: 15 mL
D: 20 mL
Correct: B
Rationale: Drug calculation: Volume to administer = (Dose required / Dose available) × Volume of stock. = (500 mg / 250 mg) × 5 mL = 2 × 5 mL = 10 mL. Always use the formula: What you WANT / What you HAVE × Volume. Double-check: 10 mL of 250 mg/5 mL suspension contains 500 mg. Verify the calculation independently, check the patient's allergy status, and use a calibrated oral syringe for liquid medications.

B: Artemether-lumefantrine (AL) for 3 days

D: Sulfadoxine-pyrimethamine (SP) single dose

Q: The composition of WHO standard oral rehydration salts (ORS) solution includes:
A: Sodium chloride, potassium chloride, sodium bicarbonate, glucose
B: Sodium chloride, potassium chloride, trisodium citrate, glucose
C: Sodium chloride, calcium chloride, sodium bicarbonate, sucrose
D: Sodium chloride, magnesium sulphate, trisodium citrate, glucose
Correct: B
Rationale: WHO/UNICEF standard ORS (reduced osmolarity, 2002 formulation): SODIUM CHLORIDE 2.6 g/L, POTASSIUM CHLORIDE 1.5 g/L, TRISODIUM CITRATE 2.9 g/L (replaced sodium bicarbonate — more stable), GLUCOSE (anhydrous) 13.5 g/L. Total osmolarity: 245 mOsm/L (reduced from 311 mOsm/L — reduces stool output and vomiting). Glucose drives sodium-glucose cotransport (SGLT1) in the intestine, facilitating sodium and water absorption even during diarrhoea. ORS is the cornerstone of diarrhoea management — prevents dehydration deaths.

ID: 4edfb7dd-b197-4853-9258-ef502e322677
Q: When administering oxytocin for induction of labour, the nurse should monitor for:
A: Hypotension and bradycardia
B: Uterine hyperstimulation and fetal heart rate changes
C: Respiratory depression and sedation
D: Hyperglycaemia and polyuria
Correct: B
Rationale: Oxytocin (Syntocinon) stimulates uterine contractions. Key monitoring during induction/augmentation: UTERINE ACTIVITY — contractions should not exceed 5 in 10 minutes (hyperstimulation/tachysystole); each contraction should last <90 seconds with adequate relaxation between. FETAL HEART RATE — continuous CTG monitoring for decelerations (late decelerations indicate fetal hypoxia). If hyperstimulation occurs: STOP the oxytocin infusion, position left lateral, give oxygen, notify the obstetrician. Also monitor maternal BP (oxytocin has antidiuretic effect — risk of water intoxication with high doses in large volumes of fluid).

ID: 21fcecad-c638-4870-8ac7-37b208afa961
Q: The vaccine given to a pregnant woman to protect her newborn against neonatal tetanus is:
A: BCG
B: Tetanus toxoid (TT)
C: DTP
D: Hepatitis B
Correct: B
Rationale: TETANUS TOXOID (TT) is given to pregnant women to protect both the mother and the newborn against tetanus. Maternal antibodies (IgG) cross the placenta and protect the neonate during the vulnerable neonatal period. Kenya MoH schedule: TT1 at first ANC contact, TT2 at least 4 weeks later, TT3 at least 6 months after TT2, TT4 at least 1 year after TT3, TT5 at least 1 year after TT4 (provides lifelong protection). Neonatal tetanus (tetanus neonatorum) results from contaminated cord cutting — a major cause of neonatal mortality in low-income settings.

B: Psychodrama

ID: eb3f9c6c-105d-47e2-a05a-be23114cc039
Q: When plotting a child's weight on a growth chart, a weight-for-age below -3 SD (Z-score) indicates:
A: Normal growth
B: Mild underweight
C: Moderate underweight
D: Severe underweight
Correct: D
Rationale: WHO Child Growth Standards Z-score interpretation for weight-for-age: Above -2 SD = NORMAL; Between -2 and -3 SD = MODERATE UNDERWEIGHT; Below -3 SD = SEVERE UNDERWEIGHT (also called severe acute malnutrition when combined with MUAC <11.5 cm or bilateral oedema). Growth monitoring using the Road to Health booklet (Kenya) plots weight monthly. Faltering growth (crossing centile lines downward) requires investigation and intervention. MUAC (mid-upper arm circumference) <11.5 cm in children 6–59 months = severe acute malnutrition.

ID: 71a379e3-f5e1-4dc3-8453-a147a19fcc13
Q: The World Health Organization recommends exclusive breastfeeding for:
A: 3 months
B: 4 months
C: 6 months
D: 12 months
Correct: C
Rationale: WHO and UNICEF recommend EXCLUSIVE BREASTFEEDING for the first 6 MONTHS of life — no other food, water, or liquids (except oral rehydration salts, vitamins, or medicines when medically indicated). After 6 months, introduce nutritionally adequate complementary foods while continuing breastfeeding up to 2 years or beyond. Benefits of exclusive breastfeeding: optimal nutrition, passive immunity (IgA, lactoferrin), reduced risk of diarrhoea, pneumonia, otitis media, SIDS, obesity, and diabetes. Q: According to the Integrated Management of Childhood Illness (IMCI) guidelines, a child aged 2–59 months with a respiratory rate of 50 breaths per minute should be classified as having:
A: No pneumonia — cough or cold
B: Fast breathing — pneumonia
C: Severe pneumonia or very severe disease
D: Normal breathing for age
Correct: B
Rationale: IMCI fast breathing thresholds: <2 months: ≥60 breaths/min; 2–11 months: ≥50 breaths/min; 12–59 months: ≥40 breaths/min. A child aged 2–59 months with RR 50 breaths/min meets the threshold for FAST BREATHING → classified as PNEUMONIA. Treatment: oral amoxicillin for 5 days, follow up in 2 days. SEVERE PNEUMONIA signs: chest indrawing, stridor at rest, inability to drink/breastfeed, convulsions, abnormally sleepy → refer urgently, give first dose of IM/IV antibiotic. No fast breathing + no chest indrawing = no pneumonia (cough or cold — symptomatic treatment only).

ID: fe62e12c-bf43-46f1-b70a-26479e9b05c0
Q: A 10-month-old child with diarrhoea has sunken eyes, a very dry mouth, and is not able to drink. This child should be classified as having:
A: No dehydration
B: Some dehydration
C: Severe dehydration
D: Mild dehydration
Correct: C
Rationale: IMCI dehydration classification: NO DEHYDRATION — no signs; SOME DEHYDRATION — two or more of: restless/irritable, sunken eyes, drinks eagerly/thirsty, skin pinch goes back slowly; SEVERE DEHYDRATION — two or more of: lethargic/unconscious, sunken eyes, NOT ABLE TO DRINK or drinks poorly, skin pinch goes back very slowly (>2 seconds). This child has sunken eyes + unable to drink = SEVERE DEHYDRATION. Treatment: Plan C — IV Ringer's lactate 100 mL/kg over 3 hours (infants) or 30 mL/kg in 30 min then 70 mL/kg in 2.5 hours. Reassess every 30 minutes.

ID: 20db74b7-3821-4e24-be61-2088efed180a
Q: Physiological jaundice in a term newborn typically appears:
A: Within the first 24 hours of life
B: Between 2–3 days of life and resolves by day 10–14
C: After 2 weeks of life
D: At birth and persists for 6 weeks
Correct: B
Rationale: PHYSIOLOGICAL JAUNDICE: appears on DAY 2–3 (never in the first 24 hours — that is always pathological), peaks on day 3–5, and resolves by DAY 10–14 in term infants (up to 3 weeks in preterm). Caused by: high fetal haemoglobin breakdown, immature hepatic conjugation (low UDP-glucuronosyltransferase), increased enterohepatic circulation. Bilirubin rarely exceeds 200 µmol/L. PATHOLOGICAL jaundice: appears <24 hours (haemolytic disease — Rh/ABO incompatibility, G6PD deficiency), bilirubin rises >85 µmol/L/day, or persists >2 weeks (conjugated — investigate for biliary atresia, hypothyroidism). Treatment: phototherapy, exchange transfusion for severe cases.

ID: 394f7dd9-7fc6-4994-8337-73c04a7582bf
Q: The recommended age for introduction of complementary foods alongside breastfeeding is:
A: 3 months
B: 4 months
C: 6 months
D: 9 months
Correct: C
Rationale: WHO/UNICEF recommend introducing COMPLEMENTARY FOODS at 6 MONTHS while continuing breastfeeding. Before 6 months, the infant's gut is immature and exclusive breastfeeding meets all nutritional needs. Starting too early increases risk of infections, allergies, and displaces breast milk. Starting too late causes growth faltering and micronutrient deficiencies. Complementary foods should be: timely (at 6 months), adequate (energy, protein, micronutrients), safe (hygienically prepared), and appropriately fed (responsive feeding). Introduce one new food at a time, starting with iron-rich foods (fortified cereals, pureed meat).

ID: b5e60853-dd82-4469-b5de-657cea828015
Q: The part of the brain responsible for coordinating voluntary movement and maintaining balance is the:
A: Cerebrum
B: Cerebellum
C: Medulla oblongata
D: Hypothalamus
Correct: B
Rationale: CEREBELLUM ('little brain') — located in the posterior fossa — coordinates VOLUNTARY MOVEMENT (smooth, precise), maintains BALANCE and POSTURE, and regulates MUSCLE TONE. Cerebellar damage causes: ataxia (unsteady gait), dysmetria (past-pointing), intention tremor, dysdiadochokinesia (inability to perform rapid alternating movements), nystagmus, dysarthria. CEREBRUM — higher functions (thought, speech, sensation, voluntary movement initiation). MEDULLA OBLONGATA — vital centres (breathing, heart rate, BP, swallowing). HYPOTHALAMUS — temperature regulation, hunger, thirst, circadian rhythm, hormone control.

ID: 27f1ccc8-ea6b-4d45-910b-78050df6402f
Q: The enzyme responsible for the initial digestion of starch in the mouth is:
A: Pepsin
B: Lipase
C: Salivary amylase (ptyalin)
D: Trypsin
Correct: C
Rationale: SALIVARY AMYLASE (ptyalin) is secreted by the parotid, submandibular, and sublingual salivary glands. It begins STARCH DIGESTION in the mouth by breaking alpha-1,4-glycosidic bonds, converting starch → dextrins → maltose. Activity continues briefly in the stomach until inactivated by gastric acid (pH <4). Pancreatic amylase continues starch digestion in the small intestine. PEPSIN (gastric) digests proteins. LIPASE (pancreatic/lingual) digests fats. TRYPSIN (pancreatic) digests proteins in the small intestine.

Q: The foremost aetiology of schizophrenia today is the:
A: Biologic perspective
B: Seasonal perspective
C: Immunologic perspective
D: Psychoanalytic perspective
Correct: A
Rationale: Current understanding of SCHIZOPHRENIA aetiology is predominantly BIOLOGICAL: (1) GENETIC — strong heritability (~80%), polygenic; (2) DOPAMINE HYPOTHESIS (excess mesolimbic dopamine → positive symptoms; deficient mesocortical → negative symptoms), glutamate (NMDA hypofunction), serotonin; (3) STRUCTURAL brain changes — enlarged ventricles, reduced cortical/hippocampal volume; (4) Neurodevelopmental — perinatal complications, prenatal infections. Psychoanalytic theories (option D) of "schizophrenogenic mother" are LONG DISCREDITED.

Q: A patient who had abdominal surgery 3 days ago develops a temperature of 38.5°C, productive cough, and decreased breath sounds at the right lung base. The most likely complication is:
A: Wound infection
B: Deep vein thrombosis
C: Post-operative pneumonia or atelectasis
D: Pulmonary embolism
Correct: C
Rationale: The "5 W's" of post-operative fever: Wind (days 1–2: atelectasis/pneumonia), Water (days 3–5: UTI), Wound (days 5–7: wound infection), Walking (days 5+: DVT/PE), Wonder drugs (any time: drug fever). On day 3 with fever, productive cough, and decreased breath sounds at the lung base, POST-OPERATIVE PNEUMONIA or ATELECTASIS (collapsed alveoli → secondary infection) is most likely. Risk factors: general anaesthesia, abdominal/thoracic surgery, immobility, pain limiting deep breathing. Prevention: early ambulation, incentive spirometry, deep breathing exercises, adequate analgesia. Treatment: physiotherapy, antibiotics if pneumonia confirmed.

ID: 3319f359-3439-473b-8883-ecdc19355971
Q: A patient with acute appendicitis has pain that started around the umbilicus and has now shifted to the right iliac fossa. This migration of pain is due to:
A: Spread of infection to the peritoneum
B: Involvement of the parietal peritoneum as inflammation progresses
C: Obstruction of the appendix lumen
D: Rupture of the appendix
Correct: B
Rationale: Classic appendicitis pain migration: INITIAL PAIN — periumbilical/central (visceral pain — poorly localised, transmitted via T10 visceral afferents from the appendix). As inflammation progresses and involves the PARIETAL PERITONEUM overlying the appendix, pain MIGRATES to the RIGHT ILIAC FOSSA (McBurney's point — 1/3 of the way from the anterior superior iliac spine to the umbilicus). Parietal peritoneal pain is well-localised, sharp, and worsened by movement. Rebound tenderness (Blumberg's sign) and guarding indicate peritoneal irritation. Rovsing's sign: pressure in LIF causes pain in RIF.

Q: The most common cause of primary postpartum haemorrhage is:
A: Retained placenta
B: Uterine atony
C: Cervical lacerations
D: Coagulation disorders
Correct: B
Rationale: PRIMARY PPH (blood loss ≥500 mL within 24 hours of vaginal delivery, or ≥1000 mL after caesarean section) causes — the 4 T's: TONE (UTERINE ATONY — 70–80% of cases, most common cause), TISSUE (retained placenta/membranes — 10%), TRAUMA (lacerations of cervix, vagina, perineum — 10%), THROMBIN (coagulation disorders — rare). ID: 48f84c31-ca8f-4ebc-8d1e-9e187e5f0b74
Q: The definitive treatment for severe pre-eclampsia is:
A: Bed rest and antihypertensive therapy
B: Magnesium sulphate infusion for 48 hours
C: Delivery of the baby and placenta
D: Corticosteroids to mature the fetal lungs
Correct: C
Rationale: Pre-eclampsia is a placental disease — the only DEFINITIVE CURE is DELIVERY OF THE BABY AND PLACENTA (removal of the trophoblast). All other treatments (antihypertensives, magnesium sulphate, corticosteroids) are supportive measures to stabilise the mother and optimise fetal maturity before delivery. Delivery timing depends on gestational age and severity: severe pre-eclampsia at ≥34 weeks → deliver; <34 weeks → consider expectant management with close monitoring in a tertiary centre. Magnesium sulphate prevents/treats seizures but does not treat the underlying disease. Antihypertensives protect against stroke but do not cure pre-eclampsia.

ID: 2c750d11-5193-4eb6-b8b5-2efee63dc4f2
Q: The first stage of labour is defined as:
A: From the onset of regular contractions to full dilatation of the cervix
B: From full dilatation of the cervix to delivery of the baby
C: From delivery of the baby to delivery of the placenta
D: From the onset of contractions to delivery of the placenta
Correct: A
Rationale: Stages of labour: FIRST STAGE — from onset of regular uterine contractions to FULL CERVICAL DILATATION (10 cm). Divided into: latent phase (0–4 cm, slow dilatation) and active phase (4–10 cm, ≥1 cm/hour). SECOND STAGE — from full dilatation to DELIVERY OF THE BABY (pushing phase). THIRD STAGE — from delivery of the baby to DELIVERY OF THE PLACENTA AND MEMBRANES (normally within 30 minutes; active management reduces to <5 minutes). FOURTH STAGE — first 1–2 hours after delivery (monitoring for PPH). The partograph is used to monitor progress in the first stage.

ID: 04902310-cf13-48c8-9071-b5a9e7b4cc7d
Q: When a cord prolapse is detected during labour, the nurse's immediate action is to:
A: Prepare the patient for immediate caesarean section
B: Manually push the cord back into the uterus
C: Elevate the presenting part off the cord with a gloved hand and call for help
D: Apply a warm saline compress to the cord and wait for the obstetrician
Correct: C
Rationale: CORD PROLAPSE management: the cord is compressed between the presenting part and the pelvis → fetal hypoxia → death within minutes. IMMEDIATE actions: (1) CALL FOR HELP (emergency team); (2) ELEVATE THE PRESENTING PART manually with a gloved hand inserted into the vagina — maintain this until delivery; (3) Position: knee-chest or Trendelenburg (gravity reduces pressure on cord); (4) Fill the bladder with 500–700 mL saline (lifts presenting part); (5) Keep the cord warm and moist (wrap in warm saline gauze — do not handle excessively); (6) EMERGENCY CAESAREAN SECTION unless vaginal delivery is imminent. Do NOT push the cord back — risk of vasospasm and injury.

Q: The direct causes of maternal mortality include:
A: Malaria, HIV/AIDS, ruptured uterus
B: Haemorrhage, sepsis, complications of induced abortion
C: Tuberculosis, anemia, malaria
D: Pre-eclampsia, haemorrhage, anaemia
Correct: B

B: Gentamycin eye drops, vit K. i.m. stat

A: Uterine action, soft tissue displacement
B: Rupture of the forewaters, expulsive uterine contractions
C: Backache, progressive cervical dilation
D: Show, erratic uterine contractions
Correct: B

Q: Following rupture of membranes during labour, drainage of golden yellow amniotic fluid could be due to:
A: Breech presentation
B: Foetal distress
C: Rhesus iso-immunization
D: Previous distress which the fetus overcame
Correct: D
Rationale: Amniotic fluid colour: CLEAR = normal; GREEN/MECONIUM-STAINED = current or recent fetal distress; GOLDEN YELLOW ("old gold") = PREVIOUS fetal distress that the fetus has survived (old meconium that has been broken down/oxidised), or chronic placental insufficiency; PORT WINE/red-brown = abruptio placentae; YELLOW with brown flecks = rhesus iso-immunisation (haemolysis). Q: When the sagittal suture is found in transverse diameter with both fontanelles palpable during vaginal examination, this is diagnostic of:
A: Face presentation
B: Brow presentation
C: Deep transverse arrest
D: Vertex presentation
Correct: C
Rationale: In DEEP TRANSVERSE ARREST (occipito-transverse position): the fetal head fails to rotate from the transverse to anterior/posterior position. On vaginal examination, the sagittal suture lies in the transverse diameter of the pelvis, and BOTH the anterior and posterior fontanelles are palpable (because the head is deflexed and neither pole has dropped below the level of the ischial spines). Q: The definite indications of elective caesarean section are:
A: Intrauterine growth retardation, antepartum haemorrhage, cephalopelvic disproportion
B: Placenta praevia type II posterior, cord presentation, diabetes mellitus
C: Fulminating pregnancy induced hypertension, diabetes mellitus, breech presentation in a primigravida

Rationale: ABSOLUTE/DEFINITE indications for elective caesarean section: PLACENTA PRAEVIA TYPE III/IV (major praevia — completely covering the os); proven CEPHALOPELVIC DISPROPORTION (CPD); transverse lie; previous classical caesarean or uterine rupture; HIGHER-ORDER MULTIPLE PREGNANCY (triplets or more); some maternal cardiac/HIV conditions; active genital HSV. ID: 3747a2bb-af5a-480c-835a-32f48d6ab6ca
Q: Presence of a hole in the membranes on examination of a placenta is indicative of:
A: Velamentous insertion
B: Succenturiate placenta
C: Bipartite placenta
D: Battledore placenta
Correct: B
Rationale: SUCCENTURIATE PLACENTA: an accessory lobe of placental tissue lies separate from the main placenta, connected by blood vessels running through the membranes. After delivery, the main placenta is delivered but the succenturiate lobe may be retained — leaving a HOLE in the membranes (the spot where the vessels passed to the accessory lobe) and vessels ending mid-membrane. This is dangerous because the retained lobe causes postpartum haemorrhage and infection — must be manually removed. VELAMENTOUS = cord inserts on membranes; BATTLEDORE = cord at placental margin; BIPARTITE = two equal lobes joined by membranes.

Q: The muscle layer of the uterus that contracts and retracts during labour to force the fetus into the receptive lower uterine segment is:
A: Circular layer
B: Middle oblique layer
C: Outer longitudinal layer
D: Endometrial layer
Correct: C
Rationale: The myometrium has 3 layers: OUTER LONGITUDINAL (subserous) — fibres run from cervix up the front, over the fundus, down the back of the uterus — these CONTRACT and RETRACT during labour, shortening progressively to expel the fetus and create the upper segment; MIDDLE OBLIQUE (figure-of-eight around blood vessels) — "living ligatures" that compress vessels after delivery to control bleeding; INNER CIRCULAR — concentrated at internal os, holds the cervix closed in pregnancy. A: Foul smelling discharge, abdominal tenderness, adnexal tenderness
B: Septic wound, odourless discharge, urinary tract infection
C: Breast infection, urinary tract infection, abdominal tenderness

ID: 7da21dae-61a8-43f5-8c4f-1fd7be32c86c
Q: The trophoblastic layer responsible for nourishing the growing organism during embedment process is:
A: Mesoderm
B: Syncytiotrophoblast
C: Cytotrophoblast
D: Ectoderm
Correct: B
Rationale: During implantation (embedment, ~6-7 days post-fertilisation), the outer SYNCYTIOTROPHOBLAST invades the maternal endometrium, erodes maternal capillaries, and forms lacunae filled with maternal blood — this provides nutrients and oxygen to the developing embryo (haemotrophic nutrition). The syncytiotrophoblast is also the hormone-producing layer. The cytotrophoblast lies underneath and provides the cellular reservoir. Mesoderm and ectoderm are embryonic germ layers, not trophoblastic.

Q: Client Mi's weight is 50 kgs at 20 weeks gestation. At 36 weeks she will weigh approximately:
A: 58 kgs
B: 60 kgs
C: 62 kgs
D: 64 kgs
Correct: B
Rationale: Expected weight gain in pregnancy averages ~0.5 kg/week from week 20 onwards. From 20 to 36 weeks = 16 weeks × 0.5 kg/week = ~8 kg gain. 50 + 8 = 58 kg minimum. The typical answer including the slightly higher rate during mid-pregnancy is ~10 kg gain = 60 kg. The exam-expected answer is B (60 kg), reflecting standard expected gain. Total pregnancy gain target ~11-16 kg for normal BMI.

A: Advice her to bring her partner for investigations, perform urinalysis
B: Exclude possibility of infection, advice on hygiene
C: Advice her to start douching, commence antifungal treatment
D: Advice her to avoid tight inner wears, commence antibacterial therapy
Correct: B
Rationale: Increased vaginal discharge in pregnancy is usually PHYSIOLOGICAL (leucorrhoea — from oestrogen-induced increased cervical mucus and vaginal transudate). Q: The importance of assessing the state of the cervix during labour is:
A: To determine adequacy of the pelvis
B: For accurate measurement of the cervix
C: To determine fetal presentation
D: To determine if the woman meets criteria for implementation of partogram
Correct: D
Rationale: Per WHO/Kenya labour management, the PARTOGRAM (partograph) is started ONCE THE WOMAN IS IN ACTIVE PHASE of labour — defined by cervical DILATATION ≥ 4 cm (older criteria) or ≥ 5 cm (current WHO 2018 update) with regular contractions. Cervical assessment is therefore the gateway to partogram use. The partogram tracks progress (cervical dilatation, descent, contractions), fetal wellbeing (heart rate, membranes, moulding) and maternal wellbeing (pulse, BP, temperature, urine) and prompts action when alert/action lines are crossed.

Q: On diagnosis of fetal distress, the fluid of choice to administer is:
A: 1 litre of 5% dextrose for 30 minutes, then evaluate foetal well being
B: 500 mls of 10% dextrose for one hour, followed by 500 mls of normal saline
C: 1 litre of normal saline for 30 minutes, then evaluate foetal heart rate
D: 50 mls of 50% dextrose as bolus then 5% dextrose for two hours
Correct: C
Rationale: INTRAUTERINE RESUSCITATION for fetal distress: position mother LEFT LATERAL (relieves caval compression), give 100% OXYGEN by mask, STOP oxytocin if running, give a CRYSTALLOID FLUID BOLUS — typically 1 LITRE OF NORMAL SALINE (or Ringer's lactate) over 20-30 minutes to improve uteroplacental perfusion. Q: Following administration of epidural anaesthesia during labour, the blood pressure should be recorded every:
A: 5 minutes for the initial 20 minutes, then at 15 minutes interval
B: 10 minutes for the first 2 hours, then half hourly
C: 15 minutes until the anaesthesia wears off
D: 10 minutes until the blood pressure reading is satisfactory
Correct: A
Rationale: Epidural anaesthesia causes SYMPATHETIC BLOCKADE → vasodilation → hypotension (common in obstetric epidurals). Standard monitoring: BP every 5 MINUTES for the first 15-20 MINUTES after epidural insertion or top-up dose (the period of maximum risk of hypotension), then every 15 minutes thereafter. Q: By 9th to 10th day postnatally the uterus is usually palpated:
A: At the umbilicus
B: Midway between umbilicus and symphysis pubis
C: Just above the symphysis pubis
D: Not palpable per abdomen
Correct: D
Rationale: Uterine INVOLUTION milestones: immediately after delivery — fundus at umbilicus; day 1 — 1 finger-breadth below umbilicus; descends approximately 1 cm (1 finger-breadth) per day. Day 7 — uterus at midway between umbilicus and symphysis pubis; DAY 10-14 — uterus has descended into the pelvis and is NO LONGER PALPABLE per abdomen. By 6 weeks postpartum it has returned to pre-pregnancy size. Persistent palpability beyond day 10-14 suggests SUB-INVOLUTION.

Q: Mrs. Wi a previous caesarean section mother para 2+0 gravida 3 comes to your health facility at 36 weeks gestation with complaints of intermittent right abdominal pain for three days. The probable diagnosis would be:
A: Ruptured abdominal ectopic pregnancy
B: Impending rupture of uterus
C: Silent rupture of the uterus
D: Acute abdomen
Correct: B
Rationale: In a multipara with previous caesarean section, scar dehiscence/rupture is a major concern. PRESENTATION: scar tenderness — INTERMITTENT or persistent pain over the previous CS scar (lower abdomen, often right side or scar line); occurs late pregnancy or during labour; if untreated progresses to uterine rupture with maternal shock, fetal distress and death. "Impending rupture" is the warning phase — the woman is alert, vital signs may be normal, scar is tender. ID: 54541a72-129c-47d6-8cb4-3f3e198db4f3
Q: Which of the following statements about twin delivery management is CORRECT?
A: After delivery of the 1st twin, if presentation is cephalic, encourage pushing — TRUE; After 2nd twin, wait for placental separation before giving uterotonic — TRUE
B: After delivery of the 1st twin, if presentation is cephalic, encourage pushing — TRUE; After 2nd twin, wait for placental separation before giving uterotonic — FALSE
C: After delivery of the 1st twin, if presentation is cephalic, encourage pushing — FALSE; After 2nd twin, wait for placental separation before giving uterotonic — TRUE
D: After delivery of the 1st twin, if presentation is cephalic, encourage pushing — FALSE; After 2nd twin, wait for placental separation before giving uterotonic — FALSE
Correct: B
Rationale: After delivery of the first twin: abdominal exam confirms longitudinal lie of twin 2; if cephalic and engaged, gentle pushing/oxytocin can establish contractions for vaginal delivery — TRUE. After delivery of the SECOND TWIN, OXYTOCIC (10 IU oxytocin IM or as IV infusion) is given IMMEDIATELY (with the anterior shoulder of the second twin or after delivery of the baby) — DO NOT wait for placental separation. Twin pregnancies have a markedly higher risk of PPH (due to overdistended uterus), so active management of the third stage with immediate oxytocics is mandatory — FALSE to wait.

Q: The immediate interventions following development of urine retention after delivery include:
A: Running tap water, sitting in a bath tub
B: Catheterization, administering analgesics
C: Applying warm compresses on perineum, catheterization
D: Sitz bath, applying warm compresses on perineum
Correct: D
Rationale: Postpartum urinary retention is common after vaginal delivery (perineal oedema/trauma, epidural effects, bladder atony). Q: The role of the midwife in relieving after pain during puerperium includes:
A: Administering appropriate analgesics, restricting ambulation
B: Encouraging bed rest, advising the mother not to breastfeed until pains reduce
C: Administering an enema, restricting ambulation
D: Encouraging frequent urination, administering appropriate analgesics
Correct: D
Rationale: Afterpains (uterine cramping during puerperal involution, particularly during breastfeeding due to oxytocin release) are managed by: APPROPRIATE ANALGESIA (paracetamol; mefenamic acid/ibuprofen are particularly effective for uterine cramps); FREQUENT URINATION (a FULL BLADDER displaces the uterus and worsens afterpains — keep bladder empty); ambulation is ENCOURAGED (improves involution, prevents DVT — not restricted); breastfeeding should CONTINUE (afterpains accompany breastfeeding but breastfeeding promotes involution). ID: 513f55aa-0c85-47bd-8bff-886e2860673f
Q: The diameter of the pelvic brim that plays a role in determining the outcome of occipito posterior position is:
A: Transverse
B: Oblique
C: Obstetric conjugate
D: Sacrocotyloid
Correct: D
Rationale: In occipito-posterior (OP) position, the fetal head occupies the SACROCOTYLOID DIAMETER (from the sacro-iliac joint to the iliopectineal eminence on the opposite side — about 9.5 cm) — narrow space. If this diameter is contracted, descent and rotation are impeded, leading to deep transverse arrest or persistent OP. The OBSTETRIC CONJUGATE (anteroposterior — sacral promontory to back of symphysis pubis, 11 cm) is the most important for ALL labours but not the specific one tested in OP. Hence the answer is D sacrocotyloid.

A: Visualization of fetus by x-ray at 16 weeks, foetal heart sound by Doppler at 5+ weeks
B: Visualization of gestational sac by trans-vaginal ultrasound at 4-5 weeks, foetal heart sound by foetal scope at 20 weeks

Q: Mrs. Y comes for her first antenatal clinic. On history taking her last menstrual period was 25.4.2011. Her expected date of confinement will be:
A: 1.2.2012
B: 2.2.2012
C: 3.3.2012
D: 4.3.2012
Correct: B
Rationale: NAEGELE'S RULE for calculating expected date of delivery (EDD): add 7 days to the first day of the last menstrual period, subtract 3 months, add 1 year. LMP = 25.4.2011. Add 7 days → 2.5.2011. Subtract 3 months → 2.2.2011. Add 1 year → 2.2.2012. By strict Naegele's rule, the correct date is 2.2.2012, option B.

Q: In the course of managing 2nd stage of labour, you realize that after delivery of the head, it tries to return in the vagina. Your immediate action in sequence would be:
A: Apply McRoberts Manoeuvre, perform episiotomy, apply supra-pubic pressure, apply Rubin's Manoeuvre, apply Woods Manoeuvre
B: Reassure the mother/partner summon for help, perform an episiotomy, change position of the mother, attempt Rubin's Manoeuvre
C: Summon for help, reassure Mother and partner, attempt change of position, apply McRoberts Manoeuvre, apply supra-pubic pressure

ID: d1e66ceb-54f9-4391-b50f-f937f79a3527
Q: Which of the following correctly describes fundal dominance in labour?
A: Contractions start at the lower uterine segment and spread upward
B: Contractions last longer and are more intense at the fundus, with the peak reached simultaneously over the whole uterus
C: Contractions are equal in intensity throughout the uterus
D: Contractions start at the fundus but are strongest in the lower uterine segment
Correct: B
Rationale: FUNDAL DOMINANCE: contractions in normal labour BEGIN at the fundus, are STRONGEST AND LAST LONGEST at the fundus, but the contraction peaks SIMULTANEOUSLY across the whole uterus — this ensures the fundus pushes downward against the relaxed lower segment to deliver the fetus. RETRACTION RING: the physiological RIDGE marking the junction between the actively contracting/retracting UPPER UTERINE SEGMENT and the passively dilated LOWER UTERINE SEGMENT. If labour is obstructed, the upper segment becomes thicker and the ring rises abnormally high — the PATHOLOGICAL retraction ring of Bandl — warning sign of impending uterine rupture.

ID: 81fd54f6-bc43-41e5-ac48-b568dd1dfc93
Q: In breech delivery the principle of hands off the breech as the baby progressively descends is to:
A: Ascertain foetal wellbeing
B: Allow enough room for the foetus to pass
C: Allow gravitating descent of the foetus
D: Avoid traction
Correct: D
Rationale: "HANDS OFF THE BREECH" (a cardinal principle of assisted vaginal breech delivery): the midwife/obstetrician does NOT pull on the baby's body once it has been delivered up to the umbilicus — TRACTION on the breech causes the head to extend, leading to head entrapment (NUCHAL ARMS) which is catastrophic. Instead the baby is allowed to deliver by maternal expulsive effort and gravity, with the operator's hands ready to perform specific manoeuvres only as needed (Lovset's for extended arms, Mauriceau-Smellie-Veit/Burns-Marshall for the after-coming head). Avoid traction — option D.

ID: 5d6a7360-4d4a-437c-bb12-d1de808387ce
Q: On abdominal examination during labour, the sinciput is felt and occiput just felt. The descent is:
A: 4/5
B: 3/5
C: 2/5
D: 1/5
Correct: D
Rationale: Descent on abdominal palpation is recorded as how many FIFTHS of the fetal head are STILL palpable ABOVE the pelvic brim (0/5 = fully descended, 5/5 = floating). When ONLY the SINCIPUT is palpable (the occiput has descended below the brim and only "just felt" or not at all), the descent is 1/5 — most of the head is below the pelvic brim and engagement is complete. Used together with vaginal exam findings (station relative to ischial spines) to monitor labour progress on the partogram.

ID: d103d09e-11fc-47a7-82c4-ce540a4880cf
Q: On vaginal examination during labour, you elicit the following: Sagittal suture is in the left oblique diameter at the pelvis, occiput points to the right iliopectineal eminence. The position of the vertex presentation is:
A: Right occipito anterior
B: Right occipito posterior
C: Left occipito posterior
D: Left occipito anterior
Correct: A
Rationale: Position is defined by the relationship of the OCCIPUT to the maternal PELVIS. The OCCIPUT points to the RIGHT ILIOPECTINEAL EMINENCE (right anterior) → RIGHT OCCIPITO-ANTERIOR (ROA) position. The sagittal suture lies in the LEFT oblique diameter (the diameter perpendicular to the line from right anterior to left posterior). ROA is a less favourable position than LOA (left occipito-anterior, the commonest position), but still permits vaginal delivery — the head usually rotates to OA before delivery.

Q: Which of the following correctly distinguishes cephalohaematoma from caput succedaneum?
A: Cephalohaematoma crosses suture lines and is present at birth; caput succedaneum is limited by suture lines
B: Cephalohaematoma is blood between the pericranium and skull, limited by suture lines; caput succedaneum is oedema of tissues superficial to the pericranium
C: Both cephalohaematoma and caput succedaneum are present at birth and cross suture lines
D: Caput succedaneum is blood between the pericranium and skull; cephalohaematoma is scalp oedema
Correct: B
Rationale: CEPHALOHAEMATOMA: subperiosteal collection of BLOOD lying between the PERICRANIUM (periosteum) and the SKULL bone — therefore LIMITED BY SUTURE LINES (does not cross them), develops over hours after birth, firm, slow to resolve (weeks-months), may cause jaundice; rarely needs intervention. CAPUT SUCCEDANEUM: OEDEMA of soft tissues SUPERFICIAL to the pericranium (scalp tissue oedema from pressure during labour) — therefore CROSSES SUTURE LINES, present AT birth, soft and pitting, resolves in 1-3 days.

Q: The Apgar score for the baby born at term with heart rate 110 bpm, gasping respiration, blue extremities, some flexion of limbs and minimal grimace at 1 minute is:
A: 10
B: 8
C: 6
D: 4
Correct: C
Rationale: APGAR scoring (each 0, 1 or 2): APPEARANCE (skin colour) — blue extremities, body pink = 1; PULSE — HR 110 (>100) = 2; GRIMACE (reflex irritability) — minimal grimace = 1; ACTIVITY (muscle tone) — some flexion = 1; RESPIRATION — gasping (slow, irregular) = 1. Total = 1+2+1+1+1 = 6. APGAR 4-6 at 1 min = moderately depressed (needs resuscitation/stimulation); APGAR <4 severe depression; APGAR 7-10 normal.

Q: The amount of 3 hourly feeds appropriate for a baby whose weight is 1600 grams on day two is:
A: 12 mls
B: 14 mls
C: 16 mls
D: 18 mls
Correct: C
Rationale: Preterm/LBW feeding (per Kenya Basic Paediatric Protocols): Day 1 = 60 mL/kg/day; Day 2 = 80 mL/kg/day; Day 3 = 100; Day 4 = 120; Day 5 = 140; Day 6 = 150; Day 7+ = 160-180 mL/kg/day. Day 2 for 1.6 kg baby = 80 × 1.6 = 128 mL/day. 3-hourly feeds = 8 feeds/day = 128 / 8 = 16 mL per feed. Answer C.

ID: fb9bb2e7-21de-4b78-845c-21f084c66627
Q: Which of the following correctly describes hereditary spherocytosis?
A: Caused by fragile red blood cells due to membrane protein defects
B: Caused by G6PD enzyme deficiency leading to oxidative haemolysis
C: Caused by abnormal haemoglobin chains
D: Caused by iron deficiency leading to microcytic anaemia
Correct: A
Rationale: HEREDITARY SPHEROCYTOSIS: defect in red-cell membrane proteins (spectrin, ankyrin) causes the RBC to assume a sphere shape — these spherocytes are osmotically fragile and trapped/destroyed in the spleen, causing haemolytic anaemia, jaundice, splenomegaly, gallstones. G6PD deficiency: X-linked enzyme deficiency; G6PD normally produces NADPH which MAINTAINS INTEGRITY of the RBC against oxidative stress; deficient cells haemolyse on exposure to oxidants (fava beans, primaquine, sulpha drugs, infection). Common cause of neonatal jaundice in Africa/Mediterranean.

A: Polycythaemia, congenital abnormalities, fetal alcohol syndrome
B: Infection, meconium aspiration, hypoglycaemia
C: Twin to twin transfusion, asphyxia, poor temperature control
D: Polycythemia, meconium aspiration, twin to twin transfusion
Correct: B
Rationale: Light-for-dates / small-for-gestational-age (SGA) babies (birth weight <10th centile) face the consequences of intrauterine growth restriction (IUGR) and placental insufficiency: HYPOGLYCAEMIA (low glycogen stores), HYPOTHERMIA (low fat, large surface area), MECONIUM ASPIRATION (chronic hypoxia → in-utero passage of meconium), perinatal ASPHYXIA, polycythaemia (chronic hypoxia → ↑erythropoietin), INFECTION (reduced immune competence), poor feeding, increased mortality. A: Room temperature of 34°C-35°C, incubator temperature 35°C-36°C

B: Cysticercus (Cysticerci)

Q: The initial symptoms of lithium toxicity include:
A: Tremors, shuffling gait, and rigidity
B: Stiff neck, occipital headache and increased blood pressure
C: Ataxia, blurred vision and severe nausea
D: Fever, sore throat and cardiac arrhythmias
Correct: C
Rationale: LITHIUM has a NARROW THERAPEUTIC INDEX (0.6-1.2 mEq/L; toxic >1.5 mEq/L). EARLY/MILD TOXICITY (1.5-2.0 mEq/L): COARSE TREMOR, nausea/vomiting/diarrhoea, slurred speech, ATAXIA, BLURRED VISION, drowsiness, muscle weakness — option C. MODERATE (2.0-2.5): confusion, dysarthria, hyperreflexia, clonus, marked ataxia. SEVERE (>2.5): seizures, coma, cardiac arrhythmias, renal failure, death. Risk factors: dehydration, NSAIDs/thiazides/ACE-inhibitors (reduce renal clearance), low sodium. Treatment: stop lithium, IV fluids, dialysis if severe.

C: Increased motor activity
D: Organised thought content and process

Rationale: ACUTE MANIA (DSM-5) — distinct period of abnormally elevated/expansive/IRRITABLE mood + INCREASED ENERGY, with ≥3 of: inflated self-esteem/grandiosity, decreased need for sleep, pressured speech, flight of ideas/racing thoughts, distractibility, PSYCHOMOTOR AGITATION/EXCESSIVE ACTIVITY, excessive involvement in pleasurable risky activities. Q: Anonymity in research is aimed at:
A: Preventing disclosure of privileged information
B: Conferring protection to the source of information
C: Selectively disclosing information to specific individuals
D: Keeping names of respondents only known to the research supervisor
Correct: B
Rationale: ANONYMITY = the researcher CANNOT LINK data back to the individual respondent — no identifying information collected or completely de-identified. PROTECTS the SOURCE/respondent from any consequence of their participation — option B. CONFIDENTIALITY = data may be identifiable but is protected from disclosure to others. Q: The study design that is appropriate in observing the behaviour changes of drug addicts under rehabilitation is:
A: Retrospective study design
B: Longitudinal design
C: Prospective study design
D: Cross-sectional design
Correct: B
Rationale: LONGITUDINAL design tracks SAME subjects OVER TIME — ideal for tracking CHANGES over the rehabilitation period (option B). PROSPECTIVE is similar but emphasises forward direction. CROSS-SECTIONAL = snapshot at one point in time — cannot show changes. RETROSPECTIVE = look back at past records. For tracking change over time during rehabilitation, longitudinal is the best fit.

ID: 655b8919-f359-45c5-a0cc-296e77d1be07
Q: The total number of new cases of HIV infection reported in the month of March from X village was 50. This is referred to as:
A: Incidence
B: Prevalence
C: Morbidity
D: Pandemic
Correct: A
Rationale: EPIDEMIOLOGICAL MEASURES: INCIDENCE = number of NEW cases of disease arising in a defined population during a specified time period (e.g. 50 new HIV cases in March in X village). Used to study disease aetiology, evaluate prevention. PREVALENCE = TOTAL number of EXISTING cases (new + old) at a point in time - useful for chronic disease burden. MORBIDITY is a broader term for disease frequency. PANDEMIC = worldwide epidemic. The question describes new cases in a defined time period = INCIDENCE.

C: Referral, nursing care, clinical care

D: Rectum
Correct: C
Rationale: TAENIASIS — infection with adult tapeworm (T. saginata — beef tapeworm, up to 10m; T. solium — pork tapeworm, up to 5m). ADULT WORM lives in the HUMAN SMALL INTESTINES (typically jejunum/ileum) — option C — attached by scolex to mucosa, releasing proglottids/eggs in faeces. Most adults are asymptomatic carriers; mild abdominal discomfort, weight loss, malnutrition possible. CYSTICERCOSIS occurs when humans ingest T. solium EGGS — autoinfection or faecal-oral. Treatment: praziquantel or niclosamide single dose.

ID: f4766e14-7fa2-4bf5-b2ee-1cc4bdd8b351
Q: The main goal of the Kenyan Government in disaster management is to:
A: Increase community preparedness
B: Take a leading role when disasters occur
C: Minimise losses and disruptions to the population and economy
D: Give early warning information on impending disasters
Correct: C
Rationale: Per Kenya's National Disaster Management Policy and the National Disaster Management Authority (NDMA), the overarching goal of disaster management is to MINIMISE THE LOSS OF LIFE, PROPERTY AND DISRUPTION to the population, economy and environment — through a CYCLE of prevention, mitigation, preparedness (option A), response (option B), recovery, and rehabilitation. Early warning (option D) is a tool within preparedness. Option C — minimising losses and disruptions — captures the OVERARCHING goal.

ID: 2d13e7e2-9cae-4fc6-b2ed-0429735ab894
Q: When using the 3-pot system for water purification, the water is considered safe for drinking after standing for:
A: 24 hours
B: 48 hours
C: 36 hours
D: 30 hours
Correct: B
Rationale: THREE-POT SYSTEM for household water treatment: water is collected and progressively settles/disinfects through three pots over 48 HOURS. Pot 1 (today) - fresh water added; Pot 2 (yesterday's water) - being used currently; Pot 3 (2 days ago / 48-hour-old water) - at the end of the day this is transferred and used; refilled each day. The 48-hour standing allows sedimentation of particles, pathogen die-off, and water becomes safer to drink. A simple, low-cost intervention for rural settings. Should be combined with chlorination, boiling or SODIS for best safety.

ID: 86986307-ebfa-4b03-a3dd-7614a2efc72b
Q: Babies of mothers on anti-TB treatment should not be immunised with BCG because:
A: Anti-TB drugs can neutralise BCG vaccine administered
B: The babies have low immunity which can make them develop tuberculosis
C: They can develop massive drug resistance
D: They are immune to TB due to maternal antibodies
Correct: A
Rationale: BCG is a LIVE ATTENUATED bovine TB strain. If administered to a baby whose mother is on isoniazid/rifampicin (or whose baby is receiving INH preventive therapy), the anti-TB drugs WILL KILL/NEUTRALISE the live BCG organisms, preventing the desired immune response — option A. Approach: DELAY BCG until the infant has completed INH prophylaxis (3-6 months), then assess and give BCG if mother is TB-negative. The baby is NOT immune from maternal antibodies (option D wrong) — TB immunity is cell-mediated, not transferable transplacentally.

ID: 03c4088a-36bd-423e-8844-131460a845b1
Q: Community mobilisation is the process:
A: In which the community is fully involved in identifying their problem and providing solutions
B: Of raising awareness on specific issues among community members
C: Of bringing members of the community together for the purpose of taking common action
D: Of moving the community members from a risky environment to a less risky one
Correct: A
Rationale: COMMUNITY MOBILISATION = a comprehensive process where the community is FULLY INVOLVED in: (1) IDENTIFYING THEIR OWN PROBLEMS (assessing needs); (2) prioritising; (3) PROVIDING SOLUTIONS (planning interventions); (4) implementing and (5) evaluating — option A captures this empowerment model. Option B is awareness-raising (a tool within mobilisation but narrower); option C is bringing together for common action (a step in mobilisation); option D misinterprets "mobilisation" literally as physical movement. The defining feature is COMMUNITY OWNERSHIP and full participation throughout.

ID: 0bd2e9a3-dbd3-4a77-84de-f49fd00d2719
Q: An example of an intervention study design is:
A: Quasi-experiment
B: Evaluative study
C: Exploratory study
D: Descriptive study
Correct: A
Rationale: STUDY DESIGNS by type: OBSERVATIONAL — researcher OBSERVES without intervening (cross-sectional, case-control, cohort); INTERVENTIONAL/EXPERIMENTAL — researcher INTRODUCES AN INTERVENTION: TRUE EXPERIMENT/RCT (with randomisation and control); QUASI-EXPERIMENT — has intervention and (often) control group but NO RANDOMISATION — option A. QUASI-EXPERIMENTAL examples: non-equivalent control group, interrupted time series, before-after with control. Useful when randomisation is unethical/impractical. EXPLORATORY, DESCRIPTIVE, EVALUATIVE are research PURPOSES, not specifically interventional designs.

ID: c4280048-1d7b-4e14-ab87-a9fd3d5627be
Q: In longitudinal research, a cohort can be defined as a sample:
A: Chosen by certain criteria and followed up for a specified period
B: With shared characteristics like age or date of birth
C: Chosen after an event
D: Representative of the whole population
Correct: B
Rationale: COHORT (epidemiology) = a GROUP OF PEOPLE WHO SHARE A COMMON CHARACTERISTIC OR EXPERIENCE within a defined time period — typically BIRTH COHORT (same year of birth), exposure cohort, occupational cohort, disease cohort. Option B captures the defining feature — SHARED CHARACTERISTICS. Cohort STUDY = follow-up of such a group over time to determine incidence of outcomes. Option A describes follow-up of a sample but doesn't capture the SHARED CHARACTERISTIC essence.

ID: 744569ee-31be-4952-9117-4792ff37f463
Q: Descriptive research:
A: Refers to the direct study of people in their natural everyday settings
B: Systematically investigates relations between two or more variables of interest
C: Involves observation for a specific purpose to test cause and effect relation between variables
D: Involves collection of data that will provide an account of individuals, groups or situations
Correct: D
Rationale: DESCRIPTIVE research = systematically COLLECTING and ORGANISING DATA to provide a detailed ACCOUNT/PICTURE of individuals, groups, situations or phenomena — answers "WHAT IS" rather than "WHY" — option D. Examples: prevalence surveys, KAP studies, case studies, situation analyses. Distinct from: ANALYTICAL/EXPLANATORY research (options B/C — examines RELATIONSHIPS and CAUSATION); ETHNOGRAPHIC research (option A — naturalistic study in cultural settings). Descriptive is foundational — basis for further analytical work.

ID: e5652214-6d8c-43c5-90dc-4b6be9f0e49d
Q: A measurable characteristic that assumes different values among subjects is referred to as:
A: Operational definition
B: Variable
C: Parameters
D: Study unit
Correct: B
Rationale: VARIABLE = a CHARACTERISTIC that can take DIFFERENT VALUES across subjects/observations (height, age, blood pressure, disease status, sex). Types: INDEPENDENT (manipulated/exposure); DEPENDENT (outcome measured); CONFOUNDING (associated with both IV and DV — distorts relationship); EXTRANEOUS; MODERATING; mediator. OPERATIONAL DEFINITION = how a variable is measured in a specific study (option A, different concept). PARAMETER = population value (vs statistic = sample). STUDY UNIT = the individual subject/element studied.

Q: The following is associated with intussusception in a 2-year-old:
A: Blood-stained stools
B: Projectile vomiting
C: Ribbon-like stools
D: Palpable mass over the flank
Correct: A

ID: e951de5e-aca8-4f26-9351-8675494f87c1
Q: The following is true concerning observation as a research method:
A: It takes less time than self-report approaches
B: It costs less money than self-report approaches
C: It is often not possible to determine exactly why the people behave as they do
D: All of the above
Correct: C
Rationale: OBSERVATION as a research method — advantages: direct, no recall bias, captures non-verbal behaviour, natural setting, suitable for those who cannot self-report. DISADVANTAGES: (1) MORE time-consuming and EXPENSIVE than self-report surveys (options A, B WRONG — observation typically MORE time/money intensive); (2) OBSERVATION captures WHAT people do but NOT WHY (the underlying motivation, beliefs, emotions) — option C TRUE; (3) Hawthorne effect; (4) observer bias; (5) ethical issues with concealed observation. Option C is the true statement; D is wrong because A and B are false.

ID: 577054db-e1d2-4547-89cf-0f6faf83cb74
Q: When constructing a questionnaire one should:
A: Use leading or loaded questions
B: Use double-barrelled questions
C: Use double negatives
D: Use multiple items to measure a single construct
Correct: D
Rationale: QUESTIONNAIRE DESIGN — what to AVOID: LEADING/loaded questions (suggest desired answer — option A — AVOID); DOUBLE-BARRELLED questions (asking 2 things in 1 — option B — AVOID); double negatives (confusing — option C — AVOID); ambiguous terms; jargon. RECOMMENDED: clear simple language; one idea per question; balanced response options; USE MULTIPLE ITEMS TO MEASURE A SINGLE CONSTRUCT (option D — TRUE) — improves reliability/internal consistency (Cronbach's alpha), captures different facets of complex concepts (e.g. depression scales use 10-21 items).

ID: c9bb3f81-f290-44ec-9cc9-9cb4ba335a66
Q: When an extraneous variable systematically varies with the independent variable and influences the dependent variable, it is called:
A: Another dependent variable
B: A confounding variable
C: A moderating variable
D: An unreliable variable
Correct: B
Rationale: TYPES OF EXTRANEOUS VARIABLES: CONFOUNDING variable — associated with BOTH the INDEPENDENT variable (exposure) AND the DEPENDENT variable (outcome), distorting the apparent relationship between them (option B). E.g. studying coffee → cancer, smoking is a confounder if smokers also drink more coffee. Must be CONTROLLED through randomisation, matching, restriction, or statistical adjustment. MODERATING variable (option C) — affects the STRENGTH/direction of the IV-DV relationship (interaction). MEDIATING variable — lies in the CAUSAL pathway between IV and DV.
----------------------------------------
ID: 3964c292-b671-4637-9626-bb6ab11e19a1
Q: The following is necessary in obtaining informed consent for research:
A: A description of the statistical analyses that will be carried out
B: A description of the purpose of the research
C: A description of the reliability and validity of test instruments
D: A list of publications that the researcher has had in the last ten years
Correct: B
Rationale: ELEMENTS OF INFORMED CONSENT (per Belmont Report, Helsinki, GCP): (1) PURPOSE of the research (option B — REQUIRED); (2) Procedures involved; (3) Duration/time commitment; (4) RISKS and DISCOMFORTS; (5) BENEFITS; (6) ALTERNATIVES; (7) CONFIDENTIALITY assurances; (8) Voluntariness — right to refuse/WITHDRAW anytime without penalty; (9) CONTACT info for questions; (10) Compensation/costs. Statistical analyses (option A), reliability/validity details (option C), researcher publications (option D) are NOT routinely required in lay-language consent forms.

Q: The following are high-level disinfectants:
A: Lysol, cidex, jik
B: Chlorine, cidex and jik
C: Bacterianios, cidex and lysol
D: Hibitate, jik and chlorine
Correct: B
Rationale: DISINFECTANT CLASSIFICATION by Spaulding: HIGH-LEVEL disinfectants (HLD) — kill ALL microorganisms EXCEPT high numbers of bacterial spores: GLUTARALDEHYDE 2% (Cidex), ortho-phthalaldehyde (OPA), HYDROGEN PEROXIDE 6-7.5%, PERACETIC ACID, hypochlorite at higher concentrations (CHLORINE/JIK at 5000 ppm or 0.5% concentration). Option B — chlorine + Cidex + Jik (concentrated) — three high-level disinfectants.

Q: Tertiary phase of cancer prevention involves:
A: Asking people to stop smoking and ingestion of alcohol
B: Regular screening of people at risk of cancer diseases
C: Early and quick treatment for those diagnosed
D: Setting facilities with ionising radiation far from settlements
Correct: C

ID: 72ba456b-00c3-443d-8cab-0959e723075e
Q: Crude birth rate (CBR) measures:
A: Number of births per 5000 population in a specified period
B: Number of newborns per 1000 mothers in a year
C: Number of births per 1000 population in a specified period
D: Number of births in 100,000 populations in a year
Correct: C
Rationale: CRUDE BIRTH RATE (CBR) = LIVE BIRTHS in 1 year / TOTAL MID-YEAR POPULATION × 1000 — option C. Reflects the OVERALL birth rate including non-childbearing population. Kenya CBR ~28/1000 (declining). GENERAL FERTILITY RATE = births / women 15-49 years × 1000 — more refined. TOTAL FERTILITY RATE = average number of children a woman would have if she went through her childbearing years at current age-specific fertility rates (TFR for Kenya ~3.4 per KDHS 2022). CRUDE DEATH RATE = deaths / population × 1000.

C: Sensitivity to light presents in early stage

Q: Self-concept comprises of the following:
A: Body image, ego, and ideal self
B: Superego, ego and ideal self
C: Self image, body image and ideal self
D: Ideal self, superego and self-awareness
Correct: A
Rationale: SELF-CONCEPT (Stuart-Sundeen/Townsend framework) — the totality of how one perceives oneself. COMPONENTS: (1) BODY IMAGE — perception of one's physical self; (2) SELF-ESTEEM — overall evaluation of self-worth; (3) ROLE PERFORMANCE — how well one performs life roles; (4) PERSONAL IDENTITY — sense of who one is; (5) IDEAL SELF — perception of who one would LIKE TO BE; (6) EGO (Freudian — the executive of personality mediating id/superego/reality). Option A — body image + ego + ideal self — captures three core components. Self-concept disturbances underlie many psychiatric conditions.
