Q: The psychological conceptual theory of psychiatric nursing assumes that:
A: Mental illness is as a result of altered levels of neurotransmitters
B: Abnormal behaviour is learnt and it can therefore be unlearnt
C: Mental illness originates from repressed feelings and experiences
D: Abnormal behaviour is as a result of poorly socialised superego
Correct: C
Rationale: CONCEPTUAL/THEORETICAL MODELS in psychiatric nursing (Townsend): (1) PSYCHOANALYTIC/PSYCHODYNAMIC (Freud, Erikson, Sullivan) — focus on UNCONSCIOUS conflicts and REPRESSED FEELINGS/EXPERIENCES from past (especially childhood) — option C. (2) BEHAVIOURAL (Skinner, Pavlov, Bandura) — abnormal behaviour is LEARNED and can be UNLEARNED (option B — behavioural model). (3) COGNITIVE (Beck, Ellis) — thoughts → feelings → behaviour. (4) BIOLOGICAL/NEUROCHEMICAL (option A — neurotransmitter imbalance). The "psychological/PSYCHODYNAMIC" approach emphasises repressed feelings/conflicts.

B: Is diagnosed when a client has had a depressed mood for more than 3 years
C: Is best managed using ECT

Q: Milieu therapy includes:
A: Providing a supportive environment for positive adaptation and personal growth
B: Teaching skills of everyday living to improve a patient's general performance
C: Use of various recreational activities to meet patient treatment goals
D: Focusing on the here and now interactions rather than repressed childhood experiences
Correct: A
Rationale: MILIEU THERAPY ("therapeutic environment/community" — Maxwell Jones) = USE OF THE PHYSICAL AND SOCIAL ENVIRONMENT OF THE WARD/UNIT as a THERAPEUTIC TOOL — providing a SUPPORTIVE, SAFE, STRUCTURED ENVIRONMENT that promotes POSITIVE ADAPTATION, recovery and PERSONAL GROWTH (option A) — captures the essence. ID: 0ecd1afc-a9cc-4594-85cd-d3d6651936f6
Q: The mode of action of antipsychotics includes:
A: Blockage of dopamine receptors in the brain
B: Stimulation of serotonin receptors in the brain
C: Blockage of adrenaline and noradrenaline receptors
D: Stimulation of dopamine receptors in the brain
Correct: A
Rationale: ANTIPSYCHOTICS — mechanism: BLOCKADE OF DOPAMINE D2 RECEPTORS in the mesolimbic pathway (responsible for positive symptoms — hallucinations, delusions) — option A. TYPICAL (first-generation) — chlorpromazine, haloperidol, fluphenazine — strong D2 blockade, more EPS (extrapyramidal side effects — Parkinsonism, dystonia, akathisia, tardive dyskinesia), hyperprolactinaemia. ATYPICAL (second-generation) — risperidone, olanzapine, quetiapine, clozapine, aripiprazole — block D2 AND 5-HT2A serotonin, fewer EPS, but more metabolic side effects (weight gain, diabetes, dyslipidaemia). NEUROLEPTIC MALIGNANT SYNDROME (NMS) — rare, life-threatening — fever, rigidity, autonomic instability, altered consciousness.

Q: The correct order of community mobilisation steps:
A: Planning, community entry, conducting, monitoring
B: Community entry, planning, conducting, monitoring
C: Making reinforcement, community entry, planning, conducting
D: Community entry, planning, monitoring, conducting

ID: 9a0f10aa-459b-49e7-8b21-1356ef401420
Q: Absorption of iron is enhanced by:
A: Antacids
B: Citrus fruits
C: Tea and coffee
D: Taking iron tablets with meals
Correct: B
Rationale: IRON ABSORPTION enhancers and inhibitors: ENHANCERS - VITAMIN C (ascorbic acid - in citrus fruits, guavas, dark green vegetables) - converts ferric (Fe3+) to ferrous (Fe2+) form which is better absorbed; ANIMAL PROTEINS (haem iron well absorbed). INHIBITORS - TEA AND COFFEE (tannins/polyphenols form insoluble complexes with iron); CALCIUM (milk, dairy, antacids); PHYTATES (whole grains, legumes); fibre; ANTACIDS (reduce gastric acidity needed for iron solubility). Counsel: take iron 1 hour BEFORE OR 2 hours AFTER meals (especially dairy/tea/coffee/antacids), with vitamin C source (orange juice).

Q: Green tags in disaster management means:
A: Victims need immediate care
B: Victims can wait up to 3 hours
C: The victims can wait up to 1 hour
D: Victims are dead
Correct: B
Rationale: DISASTER TRIAGE TAGS (START system): RED = IMMEDIATE - life-threatening but salvageable - treat first; YELLOW = DELAYED - serious but stable, can WAIT UP TO 1 HOUR for treatment; GREEN = MINOR/WALKING WOUNDED - can wait several hours (commonly up to 3 hours per some protocols) - least urgent; BLACK = EXPECTANT/DECEASED - no signs of life, or unsurvivable injuries given current resources. Option B - 3-hour green-tag waiting time - is a common quoted figure for minor injuries.

A: Planning with the stakeholders
B: Gathering information
C: Creating awareness
D: Establishing partnerships between teachers and health workers

Option B - information-gathering - should always precede planning.

Q: The type of immunity a child acquires after receiving measles vaccine is:
A: Humoral immunity
B: Artificial active immunity
C: Natural active immunity
D: Innate immunity
Correct: B
Rationale: Types of acquired immunity: NATURAL ACTIVE — from infection (e.g. having measles); NATURAL PASSIVE — transplacental IgG, breast milk; ARTIFICIAL ACTIVE — VACCINATION (immune system makes its own antibodies in response to vaccine antigen) — applies to measles vaccine — option B; ARTIFICIAL PASSIVE — immunoglobulin (e.g. rabies, tetanus immunoglobulin). ID: 71467f3d-f5b0-4ebe-8e30-8faf2c9b5831
Q: Vaccine potency can be maintained in the refrigerator by:
A: Placing vaccines in the correct compartments, defrosting the refrigerator regularly, ensuring the maintenance of ideal temperature of 0° to 8°C
B: Defrosting the refrigerator regularly, packing vaccines with enough space in-between to allow circulation of air, opening refrigerator only when necessary
C: Avoiding contact of vaccines with evaporator, regulating the thermostat regularly, lining frozen icepacks in the inner walls of the refrigerator
D: Ensuring the maintenance of ideal temperature of between +2° to +8°C, lining frozen icepacks in the inner walls of the refrigerator, placing vaccines in the correct compartments
Correct: D
Rationale: KEPI cold-chain standards: vaccines stored at +2 to +8°C in the refrigerator (NOT 0-8°C — freezing damages DPT, Hib, Hepatitis B, Pentavalent, PCV); ice packs lined in inner walls (cold-life buffer for power outages); BCG and measles in TOP shelf; OPV in middle; DPT/HepB/Penta on lower shelf (AWAY from evaporator/freezer); cold-chain monitor (VVM); never re-freeze freeze-sensitive vaccines (Shake test). Option D lists the correct temperature range (+2 to +8°C) with proper storage practices.

Q: Deficiency of vitamin B1 (Thiamine) leads to:

C: Night blindness, heart failure, impaired sensory perception

Q: The principle of unity of command in any organisation is important because:
A: The superior is able to assign part of his job domain to his/her subordinate, thus avoids overworking
B: It avoids divided loyalty since each employee receives orders from one superior
C: Employees are self-disciplined and self-controlled, so require very little supervision
D: It ensures smooth working at all levels of the organisation
Correct: B
Rationale: Fayol's 14 PRINCIPLES OF MANAGEMENT: UNITY OF COMMAND = each employee should receive orders from ONLY ONE SUPERIOR — avoids conflicts, divided loyalty, confusion, contradictory instructions. Distinct from UNITY OF DIRECTION = all activities working toward same objective should have one head and one plan. Option B captures the essence — single line of authority preserves accountability and clear loyalty. This principle is fundamental to classical management theory and remains relevant in nursing management.

ID: 89d6a9db-5077-4e20-8e01-19be4ded7505
Q: Which of the following is correct? (a) According to Douglas McGregor's Theory X, an employee will exercise self-discipline and self-control in certain circumstances. (b) When reprimanding an employee, the supervisor should avoid arguments and listen carefully to all comments.
A: (a) TRUE and (b) TRUE
B: (a) FALSE and (b) TRUE
C: (a) TRUE and (b) FALSE
D: (a) FALSE and (b) FALSE
Correct: B
Rationale: (a) FALSE — McGregor's THEORY X assumes employees DISLIKE work, AVOID responsibility, NEED COERCION, threats and CLOSE SUPERVISION (because they will NOT exercise self-discipline). THEORY Y is the opposite — assumes employees enjoy work, are self-motivated, seek responsibility and exercise self-control. So self-discipline belongs to Theory Y, not X. (b) TRUE — effective reprimanding: be private, factual, prompt, listen carefully to the employee's perspective, avoid arguments, focus on behaviour not personality, end positively. Option B: (a) FALSE and (b) TRUE.

ID: 0e691634-39d0-4477-b9d4-634ea113269b
Q: Methods of food preservation which inhibit multiplication of bacteria include:
A: Smoking, pasteurisation, salting
B: Pasteurisation, canning, salting
C: Canning, blanching, drying
D: Drying, freezing, smoking
Correct: D
Rationale: Food preservation methods: KILL bacteria — pasteurisation, canning, irradiation, sterilisation. INHIBIT MULTIPLICATION (bacteriostatic) — DRYING (removes water — pathogens need water), FREEZING (low temperature halts enzymatic and microbial activity), SMOKING (combination of dehydration and antimicrobial chemicals from smoke); SALTING (high osmotic pressure draws water out of microbes). Blanching is a brief pre-treatment, not preservation per se. Option D — all three INHIBIT multiplication without necessarily killing. Pasteurisation and canning KILL bacteria, so options A, B, C are incorrect.

Q: The goals of triaging in disaster management are:
A: Early patient assessment, control infection, determination of urgency of need of care
B: Document initial patient assessment, teach health care to the patient, notify care providers of incoming patients
C: Obtain past history records if needed, teach health care to patient, control infection
D: Brief overall patient assessment, control the flow of patients and visitors, take phone calls regarding incoming patients
Correct: A
Rationale: TRIAGE (French for "to sort") in disasters/emergencies: rapid INITIAL ASSESSMENT of all incoming casualties, DETERMINING URGENCY of treatment, prioritising those most likely to benefit from limited resources. Standard triage categories (e.g. START): RED (immediate, life threats); YELLOW (delayed — serious but stable); GREEN (minor — "walking wounded"); BLACK (expectant/deceased). Also includes infection control, identification and documentation. Option A — early patient assessment + infection control + determination of urgency — captures the three core goals.

Q: A tooth cavity that gets close to or reaches the pulp causes:
A: Pain only when one drinks something cold or eats candy
B: Sensitivity to the tooth when one bites
C: Lingering pain even after a stimulus (e.g. cold water) is removed
D: Swelling due to pus in the adjacent gum
Correct: C
Rationale: Dental caries progression: ENAMEL caries (superficial) = no symptoms or transient cold/sweet sensitivity that resolves immediately. DENTIN caries = sharper pain to cold/sweet, brief, resolves on removal of stimulus. ID: bcb6546d-0777-43d4-b870-2dd66ad8e234
Q: Incidence rate:
A: Is used to record protracted diseases
B: Shows the magnitude/burden of disease in the community
C: Is useful for planning and administration of health care services
D: Shows the rate of development of disease in the community
Correct: D
Rationale: INCIDENCE RATE = (number of NEW cases occurring in a defined population over a specified time period) / (population at risk during that period). Measures the RATE OF DEVELOPMENT of new disease cases — useful for ACUTE diseases, outbreak detection, and epidemiology research. PREVALENCE (option B) = total cases existing at a point — shows burden — useful for chronic diseases and service planning. Incidence is preferred for studying causation and acute disease dynamics.

Q: A special consideration for immunocompromised persons regarding immunisation is that:
A: Asymptomatic HIV-infected children should not receive BCG vaccine
B: Live vaccines can safely be given to persons on corticosteroid therapy
C: Asymptomatic children infected with HIV should receive all standard KEPI vaccines
D: Pentavalent vaccine can be administered to a child with neurologic disease so long as they are monitored closely

Q: When constructing a septic tank for 15 users, the recommended dimensions are:
A: Length 300 cm, Breadth 60 cm, Width 150 cm
B: Length 200 cm, Breadth 60 cm, Width 100 cm
C: Length 360 cm, Breadth 60 cm, Width 160 cm
D: Length 450 cm, Breadth 60 cm, Width 150 cm
Correct: A
Rationale: Per Kenya public health/environmental health design standards, a septic tank for 15 users is typically designed using formulae based on daily sewage production (~120 L/person) and required retention time (~24 hours), plus sludge storage capacity. Standard dimensions for ~15-user household septic tank: LENGTH ~3 m (300 cm), BREADTH ~0.6 m (60 cm), DEPTH/WIDTH ~1.5 m (150 cm). Length:breadth ratio typically 2:1 to 3:1; depth 1.5-2 m. Two-chamber design for solids settling and liquid effluent.

B: Vitamin B deficiency
C: Vitamin C deficiency
D: Vitamin D deficiency

Q: Measurement of the mid-upper arm circumference is performed:
A: When the child's age is under one year
B: With or without a weighing scale
C: As a screening method for in-patients
D: During field surveys
Correct: D
Rationale: MUAC (mid-upper arm circumference) is a quick, simple, low-tech NUTRITIONAL SCREENING tool. Best used in CHILDREN 6-59 MONTHS for screening acute malnutrition. ID: 3869047d-ea39-4478-8a24-c4cdba3bacf1
Q: When managing accidental exposure to body fluids and possible HIV infection after a needle prick, the action that should be taken in correct sequence is:
A: Wash the wound under running water with soap, take emergency ARVs, undergo HIV test
B: Take emergency ARVs, wash the wound under running water with soap, undergo HIV test
C: Wash the wound under running water with soap, undergo HIV test, take emergency ARVs
D: Undergo HIV test, wash the wound under running water with soap, take emergency ARVs
Correct: C
Rationale: Post-Exposure Prophylaxis (PEP) protocol for needlestick injury: (1) IMMEDIATE FIRST AID — encourage bleeding (don't squeeze tissue), WASH the wound thoroughly with SOAP AND RUNNING WATER for several minutes; irrigate eyes with saline if eye exposure; (2) RISK ASSESSMENT — type of exposure, source patient HIV/HBV/HCV status; (3) Source patient HIV test (with consent); EXPOSED PERSON BASELINE HIV TEST; (4) Start PEP if indicated (within 72 hours, ideally 1-2 hours) — TDF/FTC + DTG for 28 days; (5) Follow-up HIV testing at 6 weeks and 3 months. Option C reflects this sequence: wash → test → PEP.

Controlling closes the feedback loop and ensures organisational goals are being met.

ID: f7612e3d-16bd-4d99-854c-5460eb4afef7
Q: Community participation in organisation of health services in ascending order includes:
A: Information, discussion, consultation, full control
B: Consultation, information, discussion, full control
C: Consultation, discussion, information, full control
D: Information, consultation, discussion, full control
Correct: D
Rationale: LADDER/CONTINUUM OF COMMUNITY PARTICIPATION (ascending — lowest to highest involvement): (1) INFORMATION (one-way — community told what's happening); (2) CONSULTATION (community asked for views — final decision elsewhere); (3) DISCUSSION/dialogue (two-way exchange, community input considered); (4) PARTNERSHIP/joint decision; (5) FULL CONTROL/empowerment (community decides and acts). Lowest = tokenism; highest = empowerment. Option D correctly orders: information → consultation → discussion → full control.

Q: Primary prevention of occupational health care hazards includes:
A: Emphasis on first aid to minimise complications
B: Dealing with substances inhaled or contracted because of employee's poor attitudes
C: General screening of non-occupational illness before a person is hired
D: Screening of the working environment to check that recommended preventive measures are put in place
Correct: D
Rationale: Levels of prevention applied to occupational health: PRIMARY = preventing disease from occurring — workplace hazard identification/assessment, ENGINEERING controls, administrative controls (job rotation, shorter shifts), PPE, employee training, ENVIRONMENTAL SCREENING/monitoring of workplace (noise, dust, chemicals — option D). ID: 7680e4e0-5713-4d00-aae0-28f707cf020c
Q: An example of a disease that does not pass from human to human is:
A: Tuberculosis
B: Diphtheria
C: Tetanus
D: Malaria
Correct: C
Rationale: Modes of disease transmission: HUMAN-TO-HUMAN (communicable) — TB (airborne droplets), diphtheria (respiratory droplets), measles, cholera, HIV, etc. ARTHROPOD VECTOR-borne (often human-to-human via vector) — malaria (anopheles), dengue (aedes), trypanosomiasis (tsetse). ENVIRONMENTAL (NOT human-to-human) — TETANUS (Clostridium tetani spores in soil/dust enter via wounds — no human-to-human transmission), botulism, brucellosis (animal source). Tetanus is the only one in the list that is acquired from the ENVIRONMENT (soil), not from another human.

ID: 345285ae-7d15-4949-92e0-0eca0fab2e92
Q: In the treatment of tuberculosis "DOT" means:
A: Patient getting TB treatment as close to home as possible
B: Patient adheres to treatment during the intensive phase only
C: Directly observed treatment with weekly monitoring
D: Patient takes every drug dosage under supervision during the intensive phase
Correct: D
Rationale: DOT = DIRECTLY OBSERVED TREATMENT — a TB treatment strategy where a trained observer (health worker or trained treatment supporter) WATCHES THE PATIENT SWALLOW EVERY DOSE of anti-TB medication, especially during the INTENSIVE PHASE (first 2 months — 2HRZE). Continuation phase (4HR) is usually self-administered with periodic follow-up. DOT improves adherence, reduces relapse, prevents drug resistance, and is one of the five elements of WHO's DOTS strategy (commitment, case detection by smear, DOT, drug supply, monitoring). Option D is most precise — every dose under supervision during intensive phase.

A: Health management team
B: The Nursing Officers
C: Medical Officer of Health
D: Clinical Officer
Correct: A
Rationale: The DISTRICT HEALTH MANAGEMENT TEAM (DHMT) is the multidisciplinary body coordinating, monitoring and supervising ALL district-level health services and activities. Chaired by DMOH but it is a team (DMOH, DNO, DPHO, DCO, DPHN, finance, pharmacy, health records, etc.) — collectively responsible. With Kenya's devolved system since 2013, equivalent bodies are at sub-county level (SCHMT) and County Health Management Team (CHMT).

Q: Triaging may best be defined as the process of:
A: Assessing all incoming casualties of a disaster
B: Initiating diagnostic measures for casualties of a disaster
C: Determining the urgency of need of care for casualties of a disaster
D: Setting priorities and assigning urgency ratings for casualties of a disaster
Correct: D
Rationale: TRIAGE = the process of SORTING/CATEGORISING casualties based on URGENCY OF NEED FOR CARE, ASSIGNING PRIORITY RATINGS and RESOURCES based on likelihood of benefit — particularly when resources are limited. Option D is more complete than C — triage includes BOTH determining urgency AND assigning priority ratings/category (red/yellow/green/black). Various triage tools: START (Simple Triage and Rapid Treatment) for adults; JumpSTART for paediatrics; SALT (Sort, Assess, Lifesaving interventions, Treatment/Transport).

ID: aab69e9b-d63d-4c9d-bb9d-d6b0b1af1ff0
Q: The acid-generating bacterium in our mouth that is the most common cause of tooth decay (dental caries) is the:
A: Streptococcus viridans
B: Neisseria catarrhalis
C: Streptococcus mutans
D: Staphylococcus aureus
Correct: C
Rationale: DENTAL CARIES (tooth decay) pathogenesis: bacterial PLAQUE (biofilm) metabolises dietary sugars producing ACIDS that demineralise tooth enamel. The primary cariogenic bacterium is STREPTOCOCCUS MUTANS — converts sucrose to lactic acid (and synthesises insoluble glucans that anchor plaque). Lactobacilli also contribute, particularly in advanced caries. Prevention: oral hygiene (brushing with FLUORIDE toothpaste, flossing); REDUCED FREQUENCY of sugar consumption; FLUORIDE (water fluoridation, varnish); fissure sealants on molars; regular dental check-ups.
----------------------------------------
ID: 92765e2f-9f37-4ec6-9bba-5306ff4f77b4
Q: The term "role" may be defined as:
A: Community's expectation of a trained person
B: Legal and moral binding of a professional
C: Capacity to run health care activities
D: Activities one is expected to perform as a professional
Correct: D
Rationale: ROLE (sociology/professional) = the SET OF EXPECTED ACTIVITIES, BEHAVIOURS and RESPONSIBILITIES that a person is expected to perform by virtue of their POSITION (e.g., nurse, doctor, teacher). Includes role expectations from self, colleagues, patients, profession, employer, society. SCOPE OF PRACTICE is the legally-defined role boundary. Role conflict, role strain, role ambiguity are challenges. Option D is the most precise professional definition. Option A is closer to a layperson's view; option B is professional accountability; option C is competence/capability.

ID: f842f678-3676-4062-805c-e72677c4ec8b
Q: Currently in Kenya the problems associated with old age are made worse by the:
A: Early retirement and meagre pension
B: Inability to acquire new marketable skills
C: Poor health, poverty and dependency
D: Disintegration of traditional social support systems
Correct: D
Rationale: Kenya's elderly population (≥60 years) is growing rapidly. PROBLEMS faced: poverty (no/inadequate pension), declining health (NCDs, sensory loss, mobility), social isolation, neglect, elder abuse, lack of geriatric services. The DISTINCTIVE CONTEMPORARY FACTOR making things worse is the DISINTEGRATION OF TRADITIONAL SOCIAL SUPPORT SYSTEMS — traditionally elders were cared for within extended family/communal structures. Urbanisation, migration, modernisation, smaller families have eroded these systems, leaving many elders without care or support. National Older Persons Cash Transfer (Inua Jamii) and the Older Persons Act 2014 aim to address gaps.

ID: c0868692-6657-4ce3-a079-9bf40cde5711
Q: A major cause of "missed opportunity" in the immunisation programme is:
A: Failure of the cold chain
B: Use of vaccines that have lost potency
C: High drop-out of immunisable target group
D: Vaccine stock out
Correct: C
Rationale: MISSED OPPORTUNITIES in immunisation (per WHO/KEPI): occasions when an eligible child/woman comes into contact with a health service but DOES NOT RECEIVE the vaccines they need. Major causes: HIGH DROP-OUT/incomplete immunisation (started but didn't return for later doses — option C); not screening for vaccination status at every contact; service contraindications/myths; provider not knowing schedule; vaccine stock-outs at the visit (option D also a cause). High dropout (especially between Penta 1 and 3, or measles) is the LEADING cause of incomplete immunisation. Strategies: defaulter tracing, every-contact screening, integrated services.

ID: 4b99d516-4cac-45c0-a9c5-8c72599de3d7
Q: The process in community diagnosis in which data is separated and categorised in order to help one understand its meaning is known as data:
A: Handling
B: Analysis
C: Cleaning
D: Presentation
Correct: B
Rationale: Data processing stages: COLLECTION → CLEANING (option C — checking for errors, missing values, inconsistencies) → ANALYSIS (option B — SEPARATING, CATEGORISING, CALCULATING, INTERPRETING data to extract meaning — descriptive statistics, comparisons, trends, hypothesis testing) → PRESENTATION (option D — graphs, tables, narratives for dissemination); HANDLING (option A — broad term, less specific). Analysis is the cognitive step transforming raw data into INFORMATION → understanding.

ID: 40bd6492-d4dd-40fb-ba04-bc0a0eb7423c
Q: Vitamins found in the watery parts of the foods we eat:
A: Vit K, Niacin, Riboflavin, pantothenic acid
B: Pantothenic acid, Thiamine, Pyridoxine, Vit E
C: Vit K, Pantothenic acid, Thiamine, Pyridoxine
D: Folate, Cyanocobalamine, Thiamine, Riboflavin
Correct: D
Rationale: Vitamins are classified by solubility: WATER-SOLUBLE — B-complex vitamins (B1 THIAMINE, B2 RIBOFLAVIN, B3 niacin, B5 pantothenic acid, B6 pyridoxine, B7 biotin, B9 FOLATE/folic acid, B12 CYANOCOBALAMINE) and Vitamin C. Found in "watery" parts of food. Not stored extensively (except B12 in liver) — daily intake needed; excess excreted in urine. FAT-SOLUBLE — A, D, E, K — found in fatty parts, stored in liver/adipose, risk of toxicity at high doses. Option D lists 4 water-soluble vitamins (folate, B12, B1, B2). Options A and C contain Vit K (fat-soluble — incorrect). Option B contains Vit E (fat-soluble — incorrect).

ID: e7c7c1e0-68f4-4d1e-bdc4-5aacfc9f43e1
Q: Essential amino acids can be defined as amino acids that:
A: Cannot be made by the body
B: Body can make if nitrogen is available
C: Are unique in their primary structure
D: Are the basic building blocks of proteins
Correct: A
Rationale: Amino acids classified by dietary requirement: ESSENTIAL (indispensable) amino acids — CANNOT BE SYNTHESISED BY THE BODY in sufficient quantities and MUST BE OBTAINED FROM THE DIET. 9 essential AAs: HISTIDINE, ISOLEUCINE, LEUCINE, LYSINE, METHIONINE, PHENYLALANINE, THREONINE, TRYPTOPHAN, VALINE. Conditionally essential — needed extra under stress/disease (e.g. arginine in burns/sepsis; cysteine/tyrosine in liver disease). NON-ESSENTIAL — synthesised from other AAs/nitrogen. Complete protein sources contain all 9 essential AAs (animal proteins, soya); plant proteins often have one limiting AA.

Q: Water-related diseases include:
A: Typhoid, malaria, cholera
B: Malaria, cholera, schistosomiasis
C: Cholera, schistosomiasis, diarrhoea
D: Malaria, schistosomiasis, onchocerciasis
Correct: D
Rationale: WHO classification of water and disease: (1) WATER-BORNE — pathogen in water — cholera, typhoid, hepatitis A, dysentery, polio (faecal-oral); (2) WATER-WASHED (water-scarce) — diseases of poor personal hygiene from inadequate water for washing — trachoma, scabies, skin/eye infections; (3) WATER-BASED — pathogen has obligate aquatic host — SCHISTOSOMIASIS (snail intermediate host), guinea worm; (4) WATER-RELATED (vector breeds in/near water) — MALARIA (anopheles in water), DENGUE, ONCHOCERCIASIS (simulium near fast-flowing rivers), filariasis, trypanosomiasis. Option D lists three vector-related/water-based diseases.

Q: When health care is geographically, financially and culturally within easy reach to the entire community the service is said to be:
A: Available to the community
B: Acceptable to the community
C: Accessible to the community
D: Affordable to the community
Correct: C
Rationale: The "4 As" framework of health service quality/access: AVAILABILITY — services exist (clinic open, drugs in stock, staff present); ACCESSIBILITY — services are within REACH — GEOGRAPHIC (distance, transport), FINANCIAL (cost-affordability), socio-CULTURAL/SOCIAL acceptability (option C — comprehensive concept); ACCEPTABILITY — services align with cultural beliefs, dignity, language; AFFORDABILITY — services not financially prohibitive. Option C encompasses all dimensions of "easy reach" — geographic, financial, cultural — by definition of accessibility (a broader concept than just affordability or acceptability alone).

ID: 9f082904-b636-4cb6-8385-419ce575d38e
Q: The purpose of a screening test is to:
A: Diagnose persons with the disease
B: Detect persons with risk factors of sub-clinical disease
C: Check whether a population is at risk of a disease
D: Collect the data for a mass campaign
Correct: B
Rationale: SCREENING vs DIAGNOSTIC TESTS: SCREENING — applied to ASYMPTOMATIC persons to IDENTIFY THOSE AT HIGHER RISK or with SUB-CLINICAL DISEASE (pre-clinical detectable phase) — does NOT diagnose, only identifies people needing further evaluation. Examples: Pap smear, VIA/VILI, mammography, HIV testing, BP screening. DIAGNOSTIC — applied to SYMPTOMATIC or screen-positive people to CONFIRM diagnosis. Wilson-Jungner criteria for good screening: important condition, accepted treatment, facilities for diagnosis/treatment available, recognisable latent/early phase, suitable test, acceptable to population.

ID: 84fce3f1-4230-4420-9eab-4b199fb9f1b3
Q: Herd immunity may be said to exist in a community when:
A: Both dead and live antigens are used to immunise
B: Vaccination is stopped in a community that has been well vaccinated
C: The incidence of the disease decreases to a very low level
D: A high percentage of people are immune to an infection
Correct: D
Rationale: HERD (community) IMMUNITY = the indirect protection of unimmunised individuals when a SUFFICIENTLY HIGH PROPORTION of the population is IMMUNE (via vaccination or natural infection), interrupting transmission chains. Required threshold (the "herd immunity threshold") varies by R0: measles (R0 12-18) requires ~95%; pertussis ~94%; polio ~80%; influenza ~70%. Protects: those who can't be vaccinated (infants, immunocompromised), vaccine failures, those refusing. Disease cases drop sharply, sometimes to elimination. Option D is the definition.

D: Utilising of only health professionals in health care delivery

Q: Tactical plans in an organisation:
A: Focus on single daily action
B: Are concerned on how to implement operational plans
C: Are of intermediate time frame
D: Are mainly developed from the major organisation goals
Correct: C
Rationale: TACTICAL PLANS are MIDDLE-LEVEL plans of INTERMEDIATE TIME FRAME (1-3 years), bridging strategic and operational. They specify HOW to implement parts of the strategic plan - option B is reversed (tactical guides operational, not vice versa). Develop FROM STRATEGIC plans (which embody major organisational goals - option D describes strategic plans). Daily action plans are OPERATIONAL not tactical (option A). The defining feature of tactical plans is INTERMEDIATE time frame - option C.

A: Autocratic
B: Democratic
C: Bureaucratic
D: Laissez-faire
Correct: C
Rationale: LEADERSHIP STYLES: AUTOCRATIC - leader makes decisions unilaterally, strict control; DEMOCRATIC/participative - involves team in decisions; LAISSEZ-FAIRE - minimal direction, team autonomy; BUREAUCRATIC - leader manages by-the-book, STRICT ADHERENCE TO RULES, REGULATIONS AND POLICIES, formal procedures - option C; transformational - visionary, inspires change; transactional - rewards/sanctions. ID: 501768a2-a71c-403f-a26d-e7243015b183
Q: Auto-infection is commonly seen in:
A: Trichuriasis
B: Enterobiasis
C: Ascariasis
D: Schistosomiasis
Correct: B
Rationale: AUTO-INFECTION (self re-infection) is commonly seen in ENTEROBIASIS (pinworm, Enterobius vermicularis): female worm migrates to perianal area at night to lay eggs, scratching transfers eggs to hands, hand-to-mouth ingestion, continuous self-infection cycle. Eggs are immediately infectious. Strongyloides stercoralis also has classic autoinfection (eggs hatch and re-invade host). Trichuriasis, ascariasis, schistosomiasis require an external maturation period before becoming infective.

Q: Drug of choice for all Schistosomal species is:
A: Oxamniquine
B: Metrifonate
C: Praziquantel
D: Niclosamide
Correct: C
Rationale: PRAZIQUANTEL is the DRUG OF CHOICE for ALL species of schistosomiasis (S. haematobium, mansoni, japonicum, intercalatum) - single oral dose 40 mg/kg (60 mg/kg for S. japonicum). ID: c2970186-1339-4fd6-ac70-cfe32c190520
Q: A disease pandemic is one that:
A: Few persons acquire over a short period of time
B: Is always present in a population
C: Occurs occasionally in a population
D: Affects many people
Correct: D
Rationale: DISEASE OCCURRENCE TERMINOLOGY: SPORADIC - occurs OCCASIONALLY/scattered (option C); ENDEMIC - habitually PRESENT in a defined area/population at expected levels (option B); HYPER-ENDEMIC - persistently high level; EPIDEMIC/outbreak - occurrence in EXCESS of expected; PANDEMIC - EPIDEMIC SPREAD OVER MULTIPLE COUNTRIES/CONTINENTS/WORLDWIDE affecting LARGE NUMBERS OF PEOPLE (option D). Examples of pandemics: 1918 influenza, HIV/AIDS, COVID-19. WHO declares pandemics.

Q: A nurse finds an oxygen saturation of 78% in a 9-year-old child with asthma; the best action to take would be:
A: Notify the physician
B: Do nothing as this is normal
C: Administer oxygen
D: Assess the child's pulse
Correct: C
Rationale: SpO2 of 78% represents severe, life-threatening hypoxaemia. The immediate priority following the ABC principle is to administer oxygen, then notify the physician. Any delay in oxygenation risks rapid deterioration and cardiac arrest. Ref: Emergency paediatric nursing; Kenya Paediatric Protocols; ABC priority framework.

Q: Symptoms in stage 2 of WHO clinical staging of HIV/AIDS for adults include:
A: Herpes zoster, seborrhoeic dermatitis
B: Oral candidiasis, pulmonary TB
C: Thrombocytopenia, pyomyositis
D: Neutropenia, sinusitis
Correct: A
Rationale: WHO HIV CLINICAL STAGING for adults: STAGE 1 - asymptomatic, generalised lymphadenopathy. STAGE 2 - moderate unexplained weight loss <10%, recurrent respiratory infections (sinusitis, otitis, pharyngitis), HERPES ZOSTER, angular cheilitis, recurrent oral ulcers, papular pruritic eruption, fungal nail infections, SEBORRHOEIC DERMATITIS - option A. STAGE 3 (advanced) - weight loss >10%, chronic diarrhoea >1 month, persistent fever, oral candidiasis, oral hairy leukoplakia, PULMONARY TB, severe bacterial infections (pneumonia, pyomyositis), thrombocytopenia <50, neutropenia - these are STAGE 3, not 2. STAGE 4 (AIDS) - wasting, PCP, oesophageal candida, extrapulm TB, Kaposi's, toxoplasmosis.

Q: Angular stomatitis in children is as a result of deficiency in:
A: Vitamin B2
B: Vitamin B12
C: Calcium
D: Thiamine
Correct: A
Rationale: ANGULAR STOMATITIS (cheilitis, perlèche) = cracking, fissuring at the corners of the mouth - classic feature of RIBOFLAVIN (VITAMIN B2) DEFICIENCY (other features: glossitis, magenta tongue, seborrhoeic dermatitis around nose/eyes, photophobia, corneal vascularisation). May also occur with iron deficiency, other B-vitamin deficiencies, Candida albicans infection, denture-related drooling. Treatment: vitamin B2 supplementation, identify and treat secondary cause (iron, candida). Sources of B2: milk, eggs, liver, green vegetables, whole grains.

Q: Gradual social change that occurs with very little or no human effort is referred to as:
A: Evolution
B: Reform
C: Diffusion
D: Revolution
Correct: A
Rationale: TYPES OF SOCIAL CHANGE: EVOLUTION - GRADUAL, NATURAL, often unintentional change over long periods (e.g. demographic transitions, technological evolution) - option A; REFORM - planned, gradual, deliberate change within existing system (e.g. educational reform, health-sector reform); REVOLUTION - RADICAL, ABRUPT change overthrowing existing order; DIFFUSION - spread of cultural traits, innovations or behaviours from one group/society to another (e.g. spread of mobile phones, dietary trends).

ID: 32fca9a0-071c-44da-9654-6bc692fa033e
Q: Identify the correct statement below concerning malaria:
A: Humans are the definitive host for plasmodium
B: Symptoms appear in the hepatic stage
C: Malignant type is caused by falciparum species
D: Artemether lumefantrine is the 1st line therapy for complicated malaria
Correct: C
Rationale: MALARIA LIFE CYCLE: the MOSQUITO (Anopheles) is the DEFINITIVE host (sexual reproduction occurs there); HUMAN is the INTERMEDIATE host (asexual reproduction). Symptoms appear in the ERYTHROCYTIC (blood) stage, NOT hepatic (silent phase). PLASMODIUM FALCIPARUM causes MALIGNANT/SEVERE/COMPLICATED malaria - cerebral malaria, severe anaemia, multi-organ failure, ARDS - option C TRUE. ARTEMETHER-LUMEFANTRINE (AL) is 1st line for UNCOMPLICATED falciparum malaria; IV ARTESUNATE is 1st line for SEVERE/COMPLICATED malaria - option D wrong.

Q: The indications for surgery in trachoma include:
A: Conjunctivitis and pannus
B: Pannus and follicles
C: Entropion and trichiasis
D: Pannus and entropion
Correct: C
Rationale: TRACHOMA (chronic conjunctivitis from Chlamydia trachomatis) progression and WHO SAFE strategy: S - SURGERY for late-stage scarring complications: TRICHIASIS (inwardly turned lashes scratching cornea) and ENTROPION (inwardly turned eyelid margin) - both threaten vision and need surgical correction (bilamellar tarsal rotation); A - ANTIBIOTICS (azithromycin mass treatment); F - FACIAL cleanliness; E - ENVIRONMENTAL improvement (clean water, sanitation, fly control). Pannus and follicles are EARLIER active stages, treated with antibiotics not surgery.

B: Immunisation, education, community rehabilitation
C: Malaria control, essential drugs supply, equitable distribution of resources

ID: 1a90f534-c660-4546-b232-6a38a55e5da6
Q: The number of deaths from typhoid divided by the total deaths in a year multiplied by 100 is an example of:
A: Case fatality rate
B: Mortality rate
C: Cause-specific death rate
D: Case-specific death rate
Correct: C
Rationale: MORTALITY RATIOS/RATES: PROPORTIONAL MORTALITY RATIO = deaths from a specific cause / TOTAL DEATHS x 100 - shows what proportion of all deaths are due to that cause (option C - cause-specific proportion); CASE FATALITY RATE = deaths from a disease / cases of that disease x 100 - measures lethality of a disease; CRUDE DEATH RATE/MORTALITY RATE = total deaths / total population x 1000; CAUSE-SPECIFIC DEATH RATE = deaths from a cause / total population x 100,000 (population-based). The question matches the proportional mortality definition - best fitting option C.

ID: 8bde9c05-e3b9-4d83-a532-71b4fcd1248b
Q: In occupational health, deployment of epileptics away from areas where they can easily injure themselves is an example of:
A: Primary prevention
B: Secondary prevention
C: Tertiary prevention
D: Mitigation
Correct: A
Rationale: Applying LEVELS OF PREVENTION to occupational health: PRIMARY = preventing injury/disease BEFORE it occurs - pre-placement matching, engineering controls, PPE, training, ENVIRONMENTAL/job modification (e.g. KEEPING EPILEPTICS AWAY FROM heights, machinery, water where they could injure themselves during a seizure) - option A. SECONDARY = early detection (medical exams, screening). TERTIARY = rehabilitation after disease/injury. Pre-placement screening and job allocation to avoid risk = PRIMARY prevention.

Q: An employee of a milk processing industry sustained a frost bite while putting milk in a deep freezer. This is an example of:
A: Biological hazard
B: Physical hazard
C: Mechanical hazard
D: Environmental hazard
Correct: B
Rationale: OCCUPATIONAL HAZARDS classification: PHYSICAL - TEMPERATURE EXTREMES (heat/COLD - including frost bite), noise, vibration, radiation, illumination; CHEMICAL - solvents, dusts, fumes, gases; BIOLOGICAL - bacteria, viruses, fungi (e.g. healthcare-associated infections); MECHANICAL - slips/falls, machinery injuries; ERGONOMIC - repetitive strain, posture; PSYCHOSOCIAL - stress, harassment. Frost bite from sub-zero temperatures is a PHYSICAL hazard - option B.

Q: The infective stage of Taenia species is:
A: Oncosphere
B: Cysticerci
C: Hexacanth
D: Rhabditiform
Correct: B
Rationale: TAENIA SAGINATA (beef tapeworm) and TAENIA SOLIUM (pork tapeworm) life cycle: HUMAN ingests undercooked meat containing CYSTICERCI (larval stage encysted in muscle), cysticerci attach to small intestine and mature into adult tapeworms, release proglottids/eggs in faeces, eggs ingested by intermediate host (cow for saginata, pig for solium), ONCOSPHERE/HEXACANTH embryo released, invades muscle, encysts as CYSTICERCI. So for HUMANS the INFECTIVE STAGE = CYSTICERCI in meat. Note: T. solium EGGS can also infect humans if ingested causing cysticercosis/neurocysticercosis.

Q: Anthropometry is the science of measuring:
A: Size and make-up of the body and specific body parts
B: Nutritional factors needed for growth and development
C: Patients' habitual intake of food and liquids
D: Physical signs and symptoms of inadequate nutrition
Correct: A
Rationale: ANTHROPOMETRY (anthropos = man, metron = measure) = science of measuring the SIZE AND PROPORTIONS of the human body - weight, height/length, MUAC, head circumference, skinfold thickness, BMI. Used for nutritional assessment, growth monitoring, screening for malnutrition (Z-scores against WHO/CDC growth standards). Distinct from: BIOCHEMICAL assessment (lab tests), CLINICAL assessment (signs/symptoms - option D), DIETARY assessment (food intake - option C - 24-hour recall, food frequency).

ID: 5613cfe0-25d6-45ee-a539-4998269ff85e
Q: A community may be defined as:
A: Individuals and groups living and interacting within certain boundaries
B: Groups of people sharing common health problems
C: Individuals sharing same boundaries
D: Families sharing common cultural beliefs
Correct: A
Rationale: COMMUNITY definitions in community health: a GROUP OF INDIVIDUALS AND/OR GROUPS LIVING IN A DEFINED GEOGRAPHICAL AREA who INTERACT and have shared interests, characteristics, values, or institutions. Key elements: people + location/boundaries + INTERACTION + sense of belonging. Communities can be: GEOGRAPHIC (village, neighbourhood), FUNCTIONAL (school, workplace), CULTURAL (ethnic, religious), VIRTUAL (online). Option A captures the people + boundaries + interaction triad most completely.

Q: Cold chain is defined as a system of:
A: Immunising children and expectant mothers with a potent vaccine in the facility
B: Maintaining vaccines in a potent state from the manufacturer to the consumer
C: Maintaining the vaccine in a potent state in a health facility
D: Transporting vaccines from the manufacturer to the consumer
Correct: B
Rationale: COLD CHAIN = the SYSTEM (people, equipment, procedures) for MAINTAINING VACCINES at the RECOMMENDED TEMPERATURE (+2 to +8 degrees C, or frozen for some) FROM MANUFACTURER to AIRPORT to CENTRAL STORE to REGIONAL/COUNTY to SUB-COUNTY to FACILITY to CHILD (point of use). All links must be maintained. Tools: refrigerators with thermometers, cold boxes, vaccine carriers, ice packs, VVMs (vaccine vial monitors), temperature charts, freeze indicators. Break in cold chain leads to loss of vaccine potency and vaccination failures.

Correct: B

D: Diarrhoea, fever, allergic dermatitis

Q: The epidemiological triad includes:
A: Agent, route of transmission, vector
B: Host, agent, environment
C: Vector, route of transmission, host
D: Host, route of transmission, environment
Correct: B
Rationale: EPIDEMIOLOGICAL TRIAD (also called epidemiologic triangle of disease causation): the THREE INTERACTING COMPONENTS necessary for disease: (1) AGENT (the cause - biological, chemical, physical, nutritional, social); (2) HOST (the susceptible individual - age, sex, genetics, immunity, behaviour); (3) ENVIRONMENT (the surroundings - physical, biological, socioeconomic, climate). Disease arises from the interaction. Public health interventions can target any vertex. Vectors and routes of transmission are sub-components of the agent-host-environment interaction.

Q: Active immunity:
A: Is acquired after several doses of a vaccine
B: Is acquired after vaccination with a live vaccine
C: Is only acquired when organisms invade the body
D: Is life long
Correct: D
Rationale: ACTIVE IMMUNITY = host's own immune system produces antibodies/memory cells in response to antigen - NATURAL (after recovery from infection) or ARTIFICIAL (after vaccination, live or killed); develops over WEEKS but is LONG-LASTING, often LIFELONG (option D - generally true for many vaccines/infections; some require boosters). PASSIVE IMMUNITY = pre-formed antibodies transferred (transplacental, breast milk, immunoglobulin injection) - immediate but SHORT-LIVED (weeks-months). Active immunity from a SINGLE dose can suffice (live vaccines often single dose - option A/B not always true) and is NOT limited to live vaccines or natural infection (options B/C too narrow).

ID: 6db58fcb-810d-4900-a171-98708ab57060
Q: The most effective method of water purification at household level in a resource-limited setting is:
A: Filtration through a cloth
B: Boiling for at least 1 minute
C: Adding a pinch of salt
D: Allowing water to settle for 24 hours
Correct: B
Rationale: BOILING is the most reliable household water treatment method — kills ALL pathogens (bacteria, viruses, protozoa including Cryptosporidium and Giardia cysts) when water is brought to a ROLLING BOIL for at least 1 minute (3 minutes at altitudes >2,000 m). Other household water treatment options: chlorination (sodium hypochlorite — effective against bacteria and viruses but not Cryptosporidium), solar disinfection (SODIS — 6 hours in clear PET bottles in sunlight), ceramic filtration. Cloth filtration removes only large particles. Settling removes suspended solids but not pathogens.

ID: 8d88b087-66ad-4789-963f-42b365e66b3f
Q: The DOTS strategy for tuberculosis control stands for:
A: Direct Observation Treatment Short-course
B: Drug Oriented Treatment Strategy
C: Directly Observed Therapy Short-course
D: Disease Oriented Treatment Supervision
Correct: C
Rationale: DOTS = DIRECTLY OBSERVED THERAPY SHORT-COURSE. The five components of the WHO DOTS strategy: (1) Government commitment to TB control; (2) Case detection by quality sputum smear microscopy; (3) Standardised short-course chemotherapy (6 months) under DIRECT OBSERVATION — a health worker or trained community member watches the patient swallow each dose; (4) Regular uninterrupted supply of quality anti-TB drugs; (5) Recording and reporting system for programme monitoring. Direct observation prevents treatment interruption and reduces drug resistance. Kenya uses the NTLD (National TB and Lung Disease) programme.

Q: A child with bilateral pitting oedema, moon face, and sparse, discoloured hair is most likely suffering from:
A: Marasmus
B: Kwashiorkor
C: Marasmic-kwashiorkor
D: Vitamin A deficiency
Correct: B
Rationale: KWASHIORKOR is severe acute malnutrition (SAM) caused primarily by PROTEIN DEFICIENCY (with relatively adequate caloric intake). Classic features: BILATERAL PITTING OEDEMA (from hypoalbuminaemia — reduced oncotic pressure), MOON FACE (facial oedema), SKIN CHANGES (flaky paint dermatosis, hyperpigmentation), HAIR CHANGES (sparse, straight, discoloured — flag sign), HEPATOMEGALY (fatty liver), misery/apathy. Q: A patient with a panic attack presents with palpitations, shortness of breath, chest tightness, and fear of dying. The nurse's priority intervention is:
A: Administer diazepam 10 mg IV immediately
B: Stay with the patient, speak calmly, and guide slow deep breathing
C: Leave the patient alone in a quiet room to calm down
D: Administer oxygen at 10 L/min via non-rebreather mask
Correct: B
Rationale: Panic attacks are intense episodes of fear with physical symptoms (palpitations, dyspnoea, chest pain, dizziness, paraesthesia, depersonalisation) that peak within 10 minutes. Priority nursing interventions: STAY WITH THE PATIENT (presence reduces fear), SPEAK CALMLY and reassuringly, GUIDE CONTROLLED BREATHING (slow diaphragmatic breathing — 4 seconds in, hold 2, 6 seconds out — reduces hyperventilation and CO2 washout that worsens symptoms). Reassure the patient they are not dying. Benzodiazepines are used for severe/refractory cases, not as first-line. Leaving the patient alone worsens anxiety.

Q: A patient with major depressive disorder is started on fluoxetine (an SSRI). The nurse should counsel the patient that:
A: The full antidepressant effect is felt within 24–48 hours
B: The medication should be stopped immediately if side effects occur
C: It may take 2–4 weeks before the full therapeutic effect is noticed
D: The medication can be taken only when feeling depressed
Correct: C
Rationale: SSRIs (fluoxetine, sertraline, escitalopram) are first-line antidepressants. Key patient education: (1) DELAYED ONSET — therapeutic effect takes 2–4 WEEKS (sometimes up to 6–8 weeks for full effect) — patients must be counselled not to stop early thinking it is not working; (2) SUICIDE RISK — monitor closely in the first 2 weeks, especially in adolescents (SSRIs can initially increase energy before mood lifts, potentially enabling suicidal plans); (3) SIDE EFFECTS — nausea, insomnia, sexual dysfunction (usually transient); (4) DO NOT STOP ABRUPTLY — taper to avoid discontinuation syndrome; (5) Take daily, not PRN.

ID: b5887803-4284-4866-96ef-58a6835317b8
Q: A patient in alcohol withdrawal develops tremors, diaphoresis, tachycardia, and seizures 24 hours after stopping alcohol. The nurse should:
A: Administer haloperidol to control the seizures
B: Administer a benzodiazepine (e.g., diazepam) as prescribed and monitor vital signs
C: Restrain the patient and wait for the seizures to stop
D: Administer naloxone to reverse the alcohol effects
Correct: B
Rationale: ALCOHOL WITHDRAWAL SYNDROME (AWS) occurs 6–72 hours after cessation in dependent drinkers. Severity: mild (tremor, anxiety, diaphoresis, tachycardia) → moderate (hallucinations) → severe (DELIRIUM TREMENS — confusion, agitation, hyperthermia, seizures — mortality 5–15% if untreated). Treatment: BENZODIAZEPINES (diazepam, lorazepam, chlordiazepoxide) are the gold standard — they cross-react with GABA-A receptors (same as alcohol), preventing and treating seizures and delirium. Thiamine 100 mg IV/IM BEFORE glucose (prevents Wernicke's encephalopathy). Naloxone reverses opioids, not alcohol.

Q: The most hygienic method of human waste disposal in a rural community without piped water is:
A: Open defecation in a designated field
B: Throwing waste into a river
C: Use of a properly constructed pit latrine
D: Burying waste in shallow pits near the homestead
Correct: C
Rationale: A PROPERLY CONSTRUCTED PIT LATRINE is the recommended sanitation solution for rural areas without sewerage. Requirements: pit depth ≥3 metres (below groundwater table), slab with a hole (prevents flies and odour), superstructure for privacy, hand-washing facility nearby. Prevents faecal-oral disease transmission (cholera, typhoid, dysentery, polio, hepatitis A, intestinal worms). Open defecation contaminates soil and water sources. Shallow burial is inadequate — flies and rain can spread pathogens. The WHO/UNICEF Joint Monitoring Programme tracks sanitation access globally.

Q: The most effective method of malaria prevention at community level in Kenya is:
A: Indoor residual spraying (IRS) with DDT
B: Use of insecticide-treated bed nets (ITNs)
C: Prophylactic chloroquine for all community members
D: Draining all water sources in the community
Correct: B
Rationale: INSECTICIDE-TREATED BED NETS (ITNs) — particularly LONG-LASTING INSECTICIDAL NETS (LLINs) — are the most cost-effective and widely recommended malaria prevention tool at community level. They kill or repel Anopheles mosquitoes (which bite mainly between dusk and dawn), protecting sleeping individuals. Kenya distributes LLINs free through ANC clinics and mass campaigns. INDOOR RESIDUAL SPRAYING (IRS) is effective but more resource-intensive. Chloroquine prophylaxis is no longer recommended due to widespread resistance. Draining water sources is useful but impractical at scale.

A: Vitamin C
B: Vitamin D
C: Vitamin A
D: Iron
Correct: C
Rationale: VITAMIN A DEFICIENCY (VAD) is the leading cause of preventable childhood blindness. Q: Ready-to-Use Therapeutic Food (RUTF) is used in the management of:
A: Moderate acute malnutrition with complications
B: Severe acute malnutrition without complications in children 6–59 months
C: Mild underweight in children under 5
D: Severe acute malnutrition with oedema requiring inpatient care
Correct: B
Rationale: RUTF (e.g., Plumpy'Nut) is a lipid-based, energy-dense paste (500 kcal/92 g sachet) used for COMMUNITY-BASED MANAGEMENT OF ACUTE MALNUTRITION (CMAM) in children 6–59 months with SEVERE ACUTE MALNUTRITION (SAM) WITHOUT COMPLICATIONS (no oedema, no medical complications, appetite present). Moderate acute malnutrition uses supplementary foods (RUSF).

ID: 20ca895c-d369-459d-82aa-47a0192eeecb
Q: The most reliable method of sterilisation for heat-stable surgical instruments is:
A: Boiling in water for 20 minutes
B: Chemical disinfection with glutaraldehyde
C: Autoclaving (steam under pressure)
D: Dry heat oven at 100°C for 30 minutes
Correct: C
Rationale: AUTOCLAVING (steam sterilisation under pressure) is the GOLD STANDARD for sterilising heat-stable items. Standard cycle: 121°C at 15 psi for 15–20 minutes (gravity displacement) or 134°C at 30 psi for 3–4 minutes (pre-vacuum). Kills ALL microorganisms including bacterial spores. Boiling (100°C) does NOT kill spores — it is high-level disinfection, not sterilisation. Glutaraldehyde 2% is high-level disinfection (kills most organisms including spores with prolonged contact ≥10 hours) — used for heat-sensitive endoscopes. Dry heat requires 160–170°C for 1–2 hours. Spaulding classification: critical items (enter sterile tissue) must be sterilised.

ID: c4c5ca34-9026-4297-9ed0-4689cf0ce1d2
Q: A nurse sustains a needle stick injury from a patient known to be HIV positive. The first action should be:
A: Report to the occupational health department immediately
B: Wash the wound thoroughly with soap and water, then report and initiate post-exposure prophylaxis (PEP)
C: Apply antiseptic to the wound and monitor for HIV symptoms
D: Squeeze the wound to express blood, then apply iodine
Correct: B
Rationale: Needle stick injury management (WHO/Kenya MoH protocol): (1) IMMEDIATELY wash the wound with SOAP AND WATER for at least 15 minutes (do not squeeze or suck — increases risk); flush mucous membrane exposures with water/saline; (2) REPORT to the occupational health officer/supervisor; (3) RISK ASSESSMENT — source patient HIV status, viral load; (4) INITIATE PEP within 72 HOURS (ideally <2 hours) — tenofovir + lamivudine + lopinavir/ritonavir for 28 days; (5) Baseline HIV test, follow-up at 6 weeks, 3 months, 6 months; (6) Document the incident. PEP reduces HIV transmission risk by ~80%.

ID: ce7f36e1-ab20-478b-8153-74b4972d55dc
Q: In a research study, a questionnaire that measures the same variable consistently across different occasions is said to have:
A: Validity
B: Reliability
C: Objectivity
D: Sensitivity
Correct: B
Rationale: RELIABILITY refers to the CONSISTENCY or REPRODUCIBILITY of a measurement instrument — it gives the same results under the same conditions on repeated occasions (test-retest reliability) or between different raters (inter-rater reliability). VALIDITY refers to whether the instrument actually measures what it is intended to measure (content, construct, criterion validity). An instrument can be reliable without being valid (consistently measuring the wrong thing). Both reliability and validity are required for a good research instrument. Sensitivity refers to a test's ability to correctly identify true positives.

ID: b2c1671b-956d-41f5-a6da-e5b293e52c28
Q: In a study where every member of the population has an equal chance of being selected, the sampling method used is:
A: Purposive sampling
B: Stratified sampling
C: Simple random sampling
D: Convenience sampling
Correct: C
Rationale: SIMPLE RANDOM SAMPLING: every member of the population has an EQUAL and INDEPENDENT chance of being selected. Methods: lottery/ballot, random number tables, computer-generated random numbers. It is the most basic probability sampling method and minimises selection bias. STRATIFIED SAMPLING divides the population into subgroups (strata) then randomly samples from each — ensures representation of subgroups. PURPOSIVE SAMPLING selects participants based on specific characteristics (non-probability). CONVENIENCE SAMPLING selects whoever is available (non-probability — highest bias). Probability sampling methods allow generalisation to the population.
----------------------------------------
ID: 59f93ed4-d163-44b7-baeb-120604cec415
Q: The lowest level of the Kenya health system that provides the first point of contact with the community is:
A: Level 2 — Health Centre
B: Level 1 — Community Health Services
C: Level 3 — Primary Hospital
D: Level 4 — County Referral Hospital
Correct: B
Rationale: Kenya's health system has 6 levels: LEVEL 1 — COMMUNITY HEALTH SERVICES (Community Health Volunteers/CHVs, Community Health Units — first point of contact, health promotion, disease surveillance, referral); Level 2 — Dispensary; Level 3 — Health Centre; Level 4 — Primary/Sub-county Hospital; Level 5 — County Referral Hospital; Level 6 — National Referral Hospital (Kenyatta National Hospital, Moi Teaching and Referral Hospital). The Community Health Strategy (CHS) operationalises Level 1 through Community Health Units (CHUs) of ~5,000 people, each served by ~50 CHVs.

ID: 562ebda2-d853-4541-843a-44997ff1f38b
Q: The process of guiding and directing the activities of nursing staff to achieve organisational goals is called:
A: Planning
B: Organising
C: Directing
D: Controlling
Correct: C
Rationale: The four functions of management (Fayol): PLANNING (setting goals and determining how to achieve them), ORGANISING (arranging resources and tasks), DIRECTING/LEADING (guiding, motivating, and supervising staff to achieve goals — includes communication, delegation, supervision, motivation), CONTROLLING (monitoring performance against standards and taking corrective action). DIRECTING is the function that involves day-to-day guidance of staff. A nurse manager uses directing when assigning tasks, giving instructions, motivating staff, resolving conflicts, and providing feedback.

ID: 3e55321b-b2ed-4a1b-bcb2-6c3c77420506
Q: A patient with chronic obstructive pulmonary disease (COPD) is receiving oxygen therapy. The nurse should administer oxygen at:
A: 6–8 L/min via face mask
B: 1–2 L/min via nasal cannula
C: 10–12 L/min via non-rebreather mask
D: 4–6 L/min via simple face mask
Correct: B
Rationale: In COPD, the hypoxic drive replaces the normal CO2-driven respiratory stimulus because chronic CO2 retention has reset the central chemoreceptors. High-flow oxygen can suppress this hypoxic drive, causing respiratory depression and CO2 narcosis. Safe target SpO2 is 88–92%. Controlled low-flow oxygen at 1–2 L/min via nasal cannula (or 24–28% Venturi mask) is the standard approach. High-flow oxygen is reserved for acute severe hypoxia with close monitoring.

ID: 952b403f-b39b-4499-a87e-c2103ae23d3e
Q: A patient is admitted with acute myocardial infarction. The priority nursing intervention is:
A: Administer aspirin 300 mg orally and establish IV access
B: Perform a 12-lead ECG and administer morphine
C: Administer oxygen, establish IV access, attach cardiac monitor, and give aspirin
D: Insert urinary catheter and monitor urine output hourly
Correct: C
Rationale: The MONA mnemonic guides initial AMI management: Morphine (pain relief, reduces preload), Oxygen (if SpO2 <94%), Nitrates (sublingual GTN for pain if BP allows), Aspirin (300 mg stat — antiplatelet). Priority nursing actions: OXYGEN to maintain SpO2 ≥94%, IV ACCESS for drug administration, CARDIAC MONITORING for arrhythmia detection, and ASPIRIN 300 mg orally (unless contraindicated). A 12-lead ECG is essential but the combined package in option C represents the correct priority bundle.

ID: 1ee1ca8f-0563-44ac-9c96-08defcc89656
Q: A patient with chronic kidney disease has a serum potassium of 6.8 mmol/L. The nurse should first:
A: Administer sodium polystyrene sulfonate (Kayexalate) rectally
B: Restrict dietary potassium intake
C: Notify the physician and prepare for cardiac monitoring
D: Administer furosemide IV
Correct: C
Rationale: Serum potassium of 6.8 mmol/L is severe hyperkalaemia (normal 3.5–5.0 mmol/L). This is a life-threatening emergency — hyperkalaemia causes fatal cardiac arrhythmias (peaked T waves → widened QRS → sine wave → VF). The FIRST nursing action is to NOTIFY THE PHYSICIAN and initiate CARDIAC MONITORING. Definitive treatment (calcium gluconate IV to stabilise the myocardium, insulin + dextrose to shift K+ intracellularly, sodium bicarbonate, dialysis) requires medical orders. Dietary restriction alone is insufficient for acute severe hyperkalaemia.

ID: 30099062-f9a5-4fb3-a3a6-0e95e697db20
Q: A patient with increased intracranial pressure should be positioned:
A: Flat (supine) with head in neutral alignment
B: Head of bed elevated 30–45 degrees with head in neutral alignment
C: Trendelenburg position to improve cerebral perfusion
D: Left lateral position to prevent aspiration
Correct: B
Rationale: Elevated ICP management: head of bed at 30–45 degrees promotes venous drainage from the brain via the jugular veins, reducing cerebral blood volume and ICP. The head must be in NEUTRAL ALIGNMENT (no neck flexion, rotation or extension) to prevent jugular vein compression. Trendelenburg (head-down) INCREASES ICP by impeding venous drainage. Flat positioning also increases ICP. Avoid hip flexion >90 degrees. Other ICP-reducing measures: osmotherapy (mannitol/hypertonic saline), controlled ventilation, avoiding hyperthermia.

ID: f5e3b04d-c8bd-4b7e-ab37-6d9bce35501b
Q: A patient with type 1 diabetes mellitus presents with confusion, diaphoresis, and a blood glucose of 2.8 mmol/L. The nurse should:
A: Administer glucagon 1 mg IM and call the physician
B: Give 15–20 g of fast-acting carbohydrate orally if the patient can swallow
C: Administer 50 mL of 50% dextrose IV immediately
D: Recheck blood glucose in 30 minutes before intervening
Correct: B
Rationale: This is symptomatic hypoglycaemia (BG <3.9 mmol/L with symptoms). The 15-15 rule: give 15 g fast-acting carbohydrate (4 glucose tablets, 150 mL fruit juice, or 3 teaspoons sugar) if the patient is CONSCIOUS AND CAN SWALLOW, then recheck in 15 minutes. If unconscious or unable to swallow, give 50 mL of 50% dextrose IV or glucagon 1 mg IM/SC. Since this patient is confused but not unconscious, oral glucose is the first-line approach. Waiting 30 minutes without treatment is dangerous.

ID: 3dbf00c7-9e00-4352-9c86-ef50671adc0e
Q: When performing a wound dressing using aseptic technique, the nurse should:
A: Clean the wound from the outer edges toward the centre
B: Use the same swab to clean the wound in multiple strokes
C: Clean the wound from the centre outward in a single stroke per swab
D: Irrigate the wound with hydrogen peroxide to remove debris
Correct: C
Rationale: Aseptic wound cleaning technique: clean from the LEAST contaminated area (wound centre) to the MOST contaminated (outer edges/surrounding skin) — this prevents introducing bacteria from the skin into the wound. Use a SINGLE STROKE per swab/gauze, then discard — never reuse a swab. Hydrogen peroxide is cytotoxic to granulation tissue and is no longer recommended for routine wound cleaning. Normal saline or sterile water is the preferred irrigant.

Q: A patient is prescribed penicillin G. Before administering the drug, the nurse's priority action is to:
A: Check the patient's renal function tests
B: Ask the patient about allergy to penicillin or cephalosporins
C: Administer a test dose of 0.1 mL intradermally
D: Ensure the patient has eaten before administration
Correct: B
Rationale: Before administering any penicillin, the PRIORITY is to assess for ALLERGY HISTORY — penicillin allergy is the most common drug allergy (affects ~10% of patients). ID: 4d6623ad-9c48-49e7-a8ff-25486bc2b85e
Q: A patient on warfarin therapy has an INR of 5.8. The nurse should:
A: Administer the next scheduled dose and recheck INR in 24 hours
B: Hold the warfarin dose and notify the physician
C: Administer vitamin K 10 mg IV immediately without waiting for physician orders
D: Double the next warfarin dose to stabilise the INR
Correct: B
Rationale: Therapeutic INR for most indications is 2.0–3.0 (mechanical heart valves: 2.5–3.5). An INR of 5.8 is supratherapeutic — significantly elevated bleeding risk. The nurse should HOLD the warfarin dose and NOTIFY THE PHYSICIAN. The physician will decide on vitamin K administration (oral or IV depending on bleeding risk), dose adjustment, and monitoring frequency. Administering vitamin K without orders or doubling the dose are outside nursing scope. Continuing the dose with a dangerously elevated INR is unsafe.

ID: 2dc76ef6-b182-4ec6-a75b-91089d50ad24
Q: A patient on digoxin therapy reports nausea, vomiting, and seeing yellow-green halos around lights. The nurse should:
A: Reassure the patient that these are expected side effects
B: Administer an antiemetic and continue digoxin as prescribed
C: Withhold the digoxin dose, check apical pulse, and notify the physician
D: Administer atropine to counteract bradycardia
Correct: C
Rationale: The symptoms described — nausea, vomiting, and VISUAL DISTURBANCES (yellow-green halos, blurred vision, photophobia) — are classic signs of DIGOXIN TOXICITY. Other signs: bradycardia, heart block, arrhythmias, confusion. Digoxin has a narrow therapeutic index (0.5–2.0 ng/mL). Actions: WITHHOLD the dose, check APICAL PULSE for 1 full minute (hold if <60 bpm), check serum digoxin level and electrolytes (hypokalaemia potentiates toxicity), and NOTIFY THE PHYSICIAN. ID: 8878a1e7-688d-4e5c-ab6d-9b47058a90cb
Q: When mixing regular insulin and NPH insulin in the same syringe, the nurse should:
A: Draw up NPH insulin first, then regular insulin
B: Draw up regular insulin first, then NPH insulin
C: Mix the insulins in a separate vial before drawing up
D: Shake both vials vigorously before drawing up
Correct: B
Rationale: The mnemonic "Clear before cloudy": draw up REGULAR (clear) insulin FIRST, then NPH (cloudy) insulin. Rationale: if NPH (which contains protamine/zinc) contaminates the regular insulin vial, it will alter the onset and duration of the regular insulin. Drawing regular first prevents this contamination. Never shake insulin vials — roll gently to mix NPH. Never mix insulin glargine (Lantus) or detemir with other insulins. ID: b7f46e59-a417-45d6-96bb-0f9525680b96
Q: A patient receiving morphine for post-operative pain has a respiratory rate of 8 breaths per minute and is difficult to rouse. The nurse should:
A: Administer the next scheduled morphine dose and monitor closely
B: Administer naloxone (Narcan) as per protocol and notify the physician
C: Reposition the patient and encourage deep breathing
D: Administer oxygen via face mask and wait for spontaneous recovery
Correct: B
Rationale: Respiratory rate <12 breaths/min with decreased level of consciousness indicates OPIOID-INDUCED RESPIRATORY DEPRESSION — a life-threatening emergency. NALOXONE (Narcan) is the specific opioid antagonist: 0.4–2 mg IV/IM/SC, repeat every 2–3 minutes as needed (max 10 mg). It reverses respiratory depression, sedation, and analgesia. ID: b11c59a4-fcb1-4d63-a0cd-a938436b97ee
Q: A patient is prescribed enalapril (an ACE inhibitor) for hypertension. The nurse should monitor for which common side effect?
A: Hyperkalaemia and dry persistent cough
B: Hypokalaemia and peripheral oedema
C: Bradycardia and constipation
D: Hyperglycaemia and weight gain
Correct: A
Rationale: ACE inhibitors (enalapril, lisinopril, ramipril) block angiotensin-converting enzyme, reducing angiotensin II and aldosterone. Key side effects: (1) DRY PERSISTENT COUGH — due to bradykinin accumulation (occurs in 10–15% of patients; if intolerable, switch to ARB); (2) HYPERKALAEMIA — aldosterone reduction decreases potassium excretion; (3) first-dose hypotension; (4) angioedema (rare but serious — stop immediately). Contraindicated in pregnancy (teratogenic — causes fetal renal dysgenesis). Monitor renal function and potassium levels.

ID: 948e175c-9546-486f-b1cc-6c71569c9a7f
Q: The normal partial pressure of oxygen (PaO2) in arterial blood is:
A: 35–45 mmHg
B: 80–100 mmHg
C: 45–60 mmHg
D: 60–80 mmHg
Correct: B
Rationale: Normal arterial blood gas values: PaO2 = 80–100 mmHg (10.6–13.3 kPa); PaCO2 = 35–45 mmHg; pH = 7.35–7.45; HCO3 = 22–26 mEq/L; SaO2 = 95–100%. PaO2 <80 mmHg = hypoxaemia; <60 mmHg = significant hypoxaemia requiring supplemental oxygen. PaCO2 35–45 mmHg is the normal range for carbon dioxide. The oxygen-haemoglobin dissociation curve shows that at PaO2 60 mmHg, SaO2 is ~90% (the "shoulder" of the curve — below this, saturation drops steeply).

A: Giardia lamblia
B: Cryptosporidia
C: Salmonella typhi
D: Trichuris trichiura
Correct: B
Rationale: Cryptosporidium parvum causes chronic, profuse watery diarrhoea in immunocompromised HIV/AIDS patients and is classified as an AIDS-defining opportunistic enteric infection. Giardia causes self-limiting diarrhoea in immunocompetent individuals. Ref: HIV opportunistic infections; MOH Kenya HIV management guidelines.

ID: 57950610-7e75-474c-8da9-6a94f93ed62b
Q: The glomerular filtration rate (GFR) in a healthy adult is approximately:
A: 25 mL/min
B: 60 mL/min
C: 125 mL/min
D: 200 mL/min
Correct: C
Rationale: Normal GFR in a healthy adult is approximately 125 mL/min (180 L/day). Of this, 99% is reabsorbed, producing ~1.5 L of urine per day. GFR is the best overall measure of kidney function. CKD staging by GFR: Stage 1 ≥90, Stage 2 60–89, Stage 3a 45–59, Stage 3b 30–44, Stage 4 15–29, Stage 5 (kidney failure) <15 mL/min. GFR is estimated clinically using serum creatinine, age, sex, and race (eGFR — CKD-EPI or MDRD equations). GFR declines with age (~1 mL/min/year after age 40).

Q: A patient in hypovolaemic shock has a BP of 80/50 mmHg, HR of 130 bpm, and cold clammy skin. The priority nursing intervention is:
A: Administer dopamine infusion to raise blood pressure
B: Establish large-bore IV access and administer IV fluid resuscitation
C: Insert a urinary catheter to monitor urine output
D: Administer oxygen via nasal cannula at 2 L/min
Correct: B
Rationale: Hypovolaemic shock results from loss of circulating volume (haemorrhage, dehydration, burns). ID: 30f80e30-4e77-46bb-96ae-795ae92d19c5
Q: A patient is scheduled for elective surgery under general anaesthesia. The nurse should ensure the patient has been nil by mouth (NBO) for:
A: 2 hours for solids and 2 hours for clear fluids
B: 6 hours for solids and 2 hours for clear fluids
C: 8 hours for solids and 4 hours for clear fluids
D: 12 hours for all food and fluids
Correct: B
Rationale: Current anaesthesia guidelines (ASA, AAGBI): SOLIDS (including milk, non-clear fluids) — minimum 6 HOURS before elective surgery; CLEAR FLUIDS (water, black tea/coffee without milk, clear juice) — minimum 2 HOURS. Rationale: WOUND DEHISCENCE with EVISCERATION (bowel protruding through the wound) is a surgical emergency. ID: 45521c00-d097-4bf1-8432-c5388009ac07
Q: A woman with preterm labour is given magnesium sulphate for the following reasons:
A: To enhance fetal lung maturity
B: To prevent seizures
C: To improve urine output
D: To inhibit uterine contractions
Correct: D
Rationale: Magnesium sulphate has dual roles in obstetrics. In preterm labour <32 weeks it is used for FETAL NEUROPROTECTION (reduces risk of cerebral palsy) and as a TOCOLYTIC to inhibit uterine contractions (it competes with calcium at the neuromuscular junction, relaxing uterine smooth muscle). In severe pre-eclampsia/eclampsia it prevents and treats seizures. In a preterm-labour context the primary action is uterine relaxation (tocolysis).

ID: f0b7c47b-ebe7-4913-a9c0-1cc295ceb3b4
Q: The reason for administering vitamin K is to:
A: Boost the immune system
B: Stimulate growth of gastrointestinal flora
C: Protect against infection
D: Prevent bleeding problems
Correct: D
Rationale: All newborns receive intramuscular vitamin K (typically 1 mg for term, 0.5 mg for preterm) within 1 hour of birth to prevent Vitamin K Deficiency Bleeding (VKDB, formerly haemorrhagic disease of the newborn). Neonates have low vitamin K stores (poor placental transfer, sterile gut, low breast-milk content) and immature hepatic synthesis of clotting factors II, VII, IX and X. Without prophylaxis, VKDB can cause life-threatening intracranial haemorrhage.

A: Spiders
B: Being embarrassed in public
C: Leaving home
D: Losing control
Correct: C
Rationale: PHOBIAS (DSM-5): SPECIFIC PHOBIA — fear of specific object/situation (spiders = arachnophobia — option A). AGORAPHOBIA — fear of being in PLACES WHERE ESCAPE WOULD BE DIFFICULT or help unavailable in event of panic — leading to AVOIDANCE/being HOUSEBOUND (LEAVING HOME — option C). Q: A client with type I diabetes mellitus who is multigravida visits the clinic at 27 weeks gestation. The nurse should instruct the client that:
A: Do weekly fetal movement counts by the mother
B: Contraction stress testing is performed weekly
C: Induction of labor is begun at 34 weeks gestation
D: Non-stress testing is performed weekly until 32 weeks gestation
Correct: A
Rationale: In diabetic pregnancies, fetal surveillance is intensified from the late second trimester. ID: cd69f8b2-5d30-453d-8405-e1941e56b6dc
Q: The following assessment finding on a patient would warrant the nurse to suspect DVT:
A: Mid-calf pain, tenderness and redness along the vein
B: Chills, fever, malaise, occurring 2 weeks after delivery
C: Muscle pain, the presence of Homan's sign and swelling in the affected limb
D: Chills, fever, stiffness and pain occurring 10 to 14 days after delivery
Correct: C
Rationale: Classic features of deep vein thrombosis include unilateral calf/leg pain, swelling, warmth, redness, and tenderness along the deep venous tract — together with a positive Homan's sign (calf pain on dorsiflexion of the foot). Note that Homan's sign is now considered unreliable on its own and is no longer routinely used because it can dislodge a thrombus. Diagnosis is confirmed by compression Doppler ultrasound. ID: cd701222-f1cd-4106-8fad-605a3be9162b
Q: The urinary symptoms that a pregnant mother will frequently experience during the first trimester would be:
A: Dysuria
B: Frequency
C: Incontinence
D: Burning
Correct: B
Rationale: Urinary FREQUENCY is one of the earliest pregnancy symptoms, occurring in the first trimester due to (i) pressure of the enlarging uterus on the bladder while the uterus is still a pelvic organ, and (ii) increased glomerular filtration rate from pregnancy hormones. Frequency abates in the second trimester as the uterus rises into the abdomen, and returns in the third trimester as the presenting part engages. Dysuria/burning suggest urinary tract infection (which must be screened for).

A: Knowledge deficit related to self care
B: Fear related to pregnancy outcome
C: Alteration in nutrition related to sodium restricted diet
D: Activity intolerance related to compromised cardiac status
Correct: D
Rationale: NYHA Class III cardiac disease means symptoms with less-than-ordinary activity. In pregnancy, blood volume rises 40–50% and cardiac output 30–50%, placing major load on a compromised heart. Strict activity restriction, careful management of fluid balance, and delivery planning are central. Knowledge and fear are addressed but are not the priority.

ID: f4606af8-e89b-4781-b674-a587c9d817d9
Q: The following statement is correct about endometriosis:
A: It often progresses to endometrial cancer
B: Implants produce hormones that mimic the ovaries
C: Trapped haematomas cause scarring and adhesions
D: There is no known familial tendency of occurrence
Correct: C
Rationale: In endometriosis, ectopic endometrial tissue responds to monthly hormonal cycles, bleeding into the surrounding tissue with each menstrual cycle. The TRAPPED BLOOD (haematomas) cannot drain, organises, and provokes intense inflammation, fibrosis, scarring and ADHESIONS, leading to chronic pelvic pain, dysmenorrhoea, dyspareunia and infertility. Ovarian endometriomas are called "chocolate cysts." Endometriosis does NOT typically progress to endometrial cancer; there IS a familial tendency (7-fold risk in first-degree relatives).

ID: 74b21cf0-bf0b-467b-b740-14910a482d91
Q: If a papanicolaou smear report is abnormal, the next step in evaluating the client for cervical cancer is:
A: A repeat pap smear
B: An endocervical curettage
C: A colposcopy
D: A conisation
Correct: C
Rationale: Following an abnormal cytology screening (Pap smear), the next investigative step is COLPOSCOPY — direct magnified visualisation of the cervix with application of 3–5% acetic acid (acetowhite areas) and Lugol's iodine (non-staining abnormal areas). Suspicious areas are then BIOPSIED for histological diagnosis. Conisation (cone biopsy) and endocervical curettage are reserved for specific lesions identified on colposcopy. Simple repeat Pap smear is not appropriate after a clearly abnormal result.

Q: Management of Bartholin's abscess includes:

B: Apply cold packs to the vulva to reduce swelling
C: Obtain a culture of any drainage for analysis
D: Having the client wear support briefs to reduce skin irritation
Correct: C
Rationale: Bartholin abscess management: WARM (not cold) sitz baths to encourage spontaneous drainage; obtaining a CULTURE of any drainage to identify the causative organism (often polymicrobial, but Neisseria gonorrhoeae and Chlamydia trachomatis must be excluded — these inform antibiotic choice and partner notification); surgical incision and drainage with marsupialisation or Word catheter placement to prevent recurrence; systemic antibiotics if cellulitis.

Q: Manifestation of hypothyroidism in the reproductive system include:
A: Increased libido, oligospermia
B: Impotence, metrorrhagia
C: Anovulation, high incidence of spontaneous abortion
D: Irregular menstrual cycles, increased libido
Correct: C
Rationale: Hypothyroidism reduces metabolic rate and disrupts hypothalamic-pituitary-gonadal regulation. In women: ANOVULATION, menorrhagia (heavy menses) initially then oligomenorrhoea, infertility, HIGH RISK OF SPONTANEOUS ABORTION/miscarriage if pregnancy occurs, and DECREASED libido. A: Sharp rise in blood pressure and epigastric pain
B: Increase in proteinuria and vomiting
C: Weight gain and epigastric pain
D: Sharp rise in blood pressure and increase in proteinuria
Correct: A
Rationale: Impending (imminent) eclampsia is heralded by warning signs reflecting end-organ involvement: SHARP RISE IN BP (often >160/110), severe persistent headache, visual disturbances (blurring, photophobia, scotomata), EPIGASTRIC/right upper quadrant pain (liver capsule stretch from hepatic congestion — HELLP), hyperreflexia/clonus, oliguria, and reduced platelets. ID: 5ae7f500-8e9d-43f6-b170-1086412e9e1c
Q: The trophoblastic cells of the developing zygote form the:
A: Fetus and amnion
B: Placenta and fetus
C: Chorion and amnion
D: Placenta and chorion
Correct: D
Rationale: After fertilisation, the blastocyst has an inner cell mass (which becomes the embryo/fetus) and an outer trophoblast layer. Q: Spermatogenesis takes place in the:
A: Prostate glands
B: Seminal vesicles
C: Epididymis
D: Seminiferous tubules
Correct: D
Rationale: Spermatogenesis (sperm formation from spermatogonia through spermatocytes, spermatids to mature spermatozoa) occurs in the SEMINIFEROUS TUBULES of the testis, supported by Sertoli cells. B: Ruptured uterus
C: Eclampsia
D: Abortion
Correct: A
Rationale: Per the Kenya Demographic and Health Survey and the WHO/MoH maternal-mortality data, the leading direct causes of maternal death in Kenya (and globally) are: (1) HAEMORRHAGE (postpartum haemorrhage in particular), (2) hypertensive disorders of pregnancy (eclampsia), (3) sepsis/puerperal infection, (4) unsafe abortion, and (5) obstructed labour. Haemorrhage remains the single biggest direct cause. Prevention focuses on active management of the third stage of labour and skilled birth attendance.
